23. **The node's budget for checkpoints and holds is divided by channel (§15.4).** A node divides its 8 MiB for frames kept for checkpoints and frames held among its checkpointing channels, each at most 2 MiB and at least 64 KiB, and a channel over its part gives the excess back at the maintenance pass (250 ms) after the part shrank. A peer that holds the keys of many channels holds many parts: it cannot take what the others need, but a node with more than 128 channels that hold anything is past what the budget can give each of them 64 KiB of, and until the excess is given back a channel within its part finds the node's bytes taken: its frames go `unchecked` (or are dropped from the hold) and `budget_starved` is counted and told in a `warn` once in 30 s. Not attributed: the budget is not divided by link or person.
24. **The bar's outcome is not in the alert (§14.7).** `Alert::ChannelForgery` and `Alert::ChannelCheckpoint` are raised at once, and the bar is asked of the node's `IngressBar` in a task of its own, so the alert says the face was asked to be barred and not that it was. The node's log says what became of it: `NOT barred` when no `IngressBar` is attached, `barred for N s`, or `could NOT be barred` with the forwarder's reason (the node's own face, a face it does not hold). The `EndReason::BadCheckpoint` text says whether a bar was asked.
25. **Evidence is bounded (§15.5).** A node keeps the last 64 pieces of evidence and at most 4 MiB of frames and proofs between them (the oldest go first), and a frame or proof over 128 KiB is kept as its length and SHA-256: a third party cannot check the sender's signature against a hash, only that a frame it is given is the one the node saw.
26. **A receiver whose first `Keep` the forwarders hold back stalls the sender's stream for a second (§14.5, §14.10).** The sender holds the stream frames of a channel (`HELLO`, binds, checkpoints) for the `Keep` of each receiver it just judged in, at most `ROUTE_WAIT` (1 s). A forwarder passes a sink's `Keep`s upstream within a burst of 8 and then one every 5 s, so the first `Keep` of up to eight receivers behind one link (a relay) reaches the sender at once. A ninth receiver that joins before the burst has paid back (one every 5 s) finds its `Keep` held, and the sender's stream frames (its checkpoints too) wait the second out; during it a `Latest` receiver defers the data frames a checkpoint has not vouched for and may drop them. Not fixed: the `UP` record carries no receiver, so a forwarder cannot tell a receiver's first `Keep` from a repeat, and a limit that did not bound the `Keep`s a sink passes would let one face make the table send one frame per `Keep` it is sent.
27. **A lost first `Keep`, or a session held longer than `ROUTE_WAIT`, drops the `HELLO` and the receiver is told `NoPath` (§14.5, §14.10).** The sender's stream gate waits at most `ROUTE_WAIT` (1 s) for the first `Keep` of a channel (§14.5). That `Keep` is one datagram, sent when the receiver installs the session and then every `KEEP_EVERY` (10 s); on a lossy network it can be lost, and on a slow one it can come later than the wait (so can the session Data that lays the route it travels). A channel whose wait ran out without any `Keep` drops the stream frame, the `HELLO` among them, and every later stream frame until a `Keep` comes. The receiver then has no frame in `NO_PATH_AFTER` (2 s) and is told `NoPath`. It cannot tell this from a forwarder that does not carry channel frames, so the refusal says both (§14.10): update the forwarder, **or** the first `Keep` or the session was lost or held up on the way and the receiver should ask again. A new ask is a new session with a new `Keep`; the host does it (the conformance cases c19-c22 do). Accepted: the protocol sends no `Keep` again before `KEEP_EVERY` and the sender waits no longer than `ROUTE_WAIT`, because the wait is what keeps a stalled receiver from holding a channel's checkpoints (item 26), and a retry inside the protocol would be a second timer every receiver pays for to cover a loss only a lossy link makes; the cost is one more ask by a receiver that was told why.
    Measured on the conformance suite's faulty profile (20 % of the arrivals dropped, over two hops each way): a receiver's setup that must ask again after each loss (an ask or answer that is lost costs the 5 s wait of the ask) takes a median of 15 s and, in about one run of three hundred, more than 120 s. A case that waits for a `HELLO` over that profile is a lottery on its seed, so c34, whose subject is a flood, runs on a lossless network.
28. **One face's share of the setup budgets is shared by whoever stands behind it (§3.3 steps 2 and 3).** The lookup and verification budgets are kept per ingress face and per node, because the face is all a sender knows of an unverified ask. A stranger that sends well-formed asks naming a listed key, with a garbage signature, can empty the budgets of **its own face** (64 verifications a second, burst 256) and so make every ask over that same face `Congestion` for as long as it floods; it cannot touch another face's, and it cannot make verifying cost more than the node's ceiling (1,024 a second). Behind one router (the hub of a simulation, a relay) the stranger and the genuine askers share a face, so they share its bucket: what keeps them apart is that router's rate limit on its own faces, not the sender's. The per-key bucket (4 a second) is charged only after the signature verified, so a forgery naming a member never costs that member a token. Not fixed: telling two sources behind one face apart needs a signature, and the signature is what the budget protects.
    Tests: ndn-heap `setup_tests` `a_flood_of_forged_asks_from_one_face_leaves_another_faces_asks_answered` (a clock that stands still: a genuine ask over another face is answered after the flood; one over the flooded face is told `Congestion` and is answered once the face's budget has refilled) and `gates` `one_faces_flood_leaves_another_faces_budget_whole`. A member's own flood over the one face of the simulation's hub does not starve a genuine asker either: her key's own bucket refuses her before a token of the face is taken (§3.3), and the conformance case c34 has another member answered while 48 loops of hers ask.
29. **A channel's places are first come, first kept (§3.5, §6).** A channel keeps 256 receivers and a person holds at most 32 of them (`RECEIVERS_PER_PERSON`), so one member with many device keys cannot fill it, but eight members that each use 32 devices can, and the next asker is refused `TooMany` until a lease runs out (a lifetime and 5 s of grace; a receiver that left is not told). **Why accepted:** evicting a receiver to seat a newcomer would let any asker push an honest receiver out, and every eviction is a re-key that makes every other receiver ask again (§3.6); who may be a member at all is the owner's decision. **Bound:** 256 places a channel, 32 a person, 64 channels a sender.
30. **A kept feed's re-read and read-ahead cost the size of what it reads (§17.5).** (a) A feed whose queue of 4,096 overflowed reads the heads under its selector again under the space's lock: one scan of every head under `what` and one read of each head that is not the entry it told, per overflow. Members changes, alerts and values of other paths are not queued (so they cannot cause it); what causes it is 4,096 **wanted** changes (values under `what`, and withdrawals, which name no path) while the reader is slow, each a signed entry of a writer member (or the withdrawal of one). Each re-read empties the queue, so a flood costs one scan per 4,096 entries per slow reader and cannot loop. (b) `listed` and `again` hold a value per head (an inline value is at most 6,144 octets, `INLINE_MAX`; a value in pieces is a reference and is read from the store, blocking, when the stream reaches it), and `delivered` one record per path told: memory O(heads under `what`) for one `Items` over a large selector. **Why accepted:** (a) needs the feed's reader to be slow and the writer to sign the entries, and the scan is what makes a slow reader lose no value; (b) holding references instead of inline values needs a store read per item under the log's lock and a second look at whether the head moved, which this stage does not take on. **Bound:** the heads of the reader's own space under its selector; a narrow selector (`Path`) holds one.
31. **`by` and `writer` of a `Prompt` emission are a key holder's claim until a checkpoint covers the frame, and for good where none does (§17.6).** The session names the sender's device and person, judged when it was set up; the frame is authenticated only as the work of a holder of the channel key, which every reader of a sealed space holds, until a signed checkpoint of the sender lists it. A `Prompt` receiver gives the item at once and cannot say afterwards that it was covered (`Emitted::verified` is the state when the item was given, so it is `false`); on a channel that sends no checkpoints (item 16) none ever covers it. **Why accepted:** holding every frame until a checkpoint lists it is `Trust::Verified`, at up to one period of latency, and is the receiver's choice (§15.8); revisiting an item given would need the item or a handle kept past the consumer's read. **Bound:** the window `W` (§15.9) on a channel with checkpoints, with the forgery exposed by a `Retracted` notice, evidence and a bar of the face (§15.5); unbounded in time, never in effect, on a channel without: read with `Trust::Verified` or read `Kept`.
32. **No padding bucket hides the size of a message from the path.** The replaced sealed-scope signals padded each payload to a 128-octet bucket; a channel pads only a sealed frame shorter than 46 octets (§11.3), so the length of a frame, and of the messages combined in it, shows to every forwarder and listener on its path, as does its timing. **Why accepted:** a bucket costs bandwidth on every frame of every channel, including the large ones the design is for, and the sizes of a message an application sends are its own choice: an application that needs them hidden pads its payload. **Bound:** the content is sealed; only lengths (at most 9,000 octets) and times are visible.
33. **No rate limits the frames a key holder sends and a receiver opens.** The replaced signals limited a run to 120 sends a second and a receiver to 240 messages a run and 2,000 a scope; the channel has no such counter. A holder of a channel key can seal frames as fast as its link carries them, and a receiver spends one AEAD attempt on each that passes the cheap checks (a routing-header parse, a table lookup by channel id, §11.8: 0.12 µs for 64 octets); a frame that is too large, too short, malformed or of an unknown channel costs none. **Why accepted:** a count by key holder is a count by a key every reader holds, so it would limit the sender along with the forger; what holds is the structure: the replay window refuses a duplicate before any cryptography, a frame more than 2^24 sequences ahead is refused (`SEQ_JUMP_MAX`, item 3), the early-frame hold, the name table (4,096 ids) and the sink queues (1 MiB) are bounded, checkpoints are verified at a bucket (a channel: a burst of 64, then 2 a period; the node: 256 then 4,000 a second, §15.4), and on radio faces a sink face's rate drops the rest at the forwarder (§14.8). **Bound:** the link rate, and on a face with a `SideRate` that rate.
34. **A stand lifetime is the sender's, up to one hour (§13.6).** `EmitOptions::stand` lets the sender keep the last emission of a path and replay it to every new receiver, with `stands` set to what is left, for up to `STAND_MAX` (3,600 s); the replaced signals' standing values lasted 60 s. A receiver refuses a lifetime above the maximum (`StandSeconds`), but a writer member can make a stale value stand for an hour. **Why accepted:** an hour is what makes a standing value useful for a presence or a position that changes rarely; the lifetime is the sender's data and every replayed item says how much is left. **Bound:** 256 names and 64 KiB a channel, one hour, and one replay a second per channel for receivers that are not new (§13.6).
35. **quinn's queue from the endpoint to a connection is unbounded; what bounds it is scheduling, not memory (§18.3, security review of the performance pass).** quinn hands each datagram, once it has found the connection by the destination connection id in the packet's cleartext header and before any decryption, to that connection through an unbounded queue (`mpsc::unbounded_channel`), which the connection's driver empties each time it runs. The queue grows only while the driver is not scheduled as fast as the endpoint's driver fills it (that driver reads in bursts of at most 50 µs and then yields), and the endpoint's and the connection's drivers run on the runtime that made the endpoint, which in the in-tree examples also runs the node's reader. In the bad mode of §18.4, a host read loop that never waited, the queue reached 506 MiB in 3 s. What bounds it now is cooperation: `deliver_frame` hands the executor back once per 64 KiB of frames, the engine's face reader yields after each side frame a sink refused, and the app face's inbox between the forwarder and the node's task is bounded (256 frames, 8 MiB), so a reader waits for the task and tokio's budget makes a task that never waits yield every 128 operations. It is **not** a structural bound: a host whose tasks hog the runtime's workers (a read loop of its own that never awaits, a single worker with a long task) can still let the queue grow. **Who can fill it:** the holder of a live connection id, that is the peer of the link and anyone on the path who sees the id (a short header carries it in the clear); a datagram that fails to decrypt costs the connection driver a failed decryption and is never delivered. **Why accepted:** the queue is quinn's, and nothing in the channel, the forwarder or `ndn-face-quic` can bound it without a patch to quinn; the exposure is the one every quinn endpoint has, and the pass made it smaller, never larger. **Bound for an operator:** give a link's endpoint a runtime of its own (`chan_relay` `relay_workers` does it for the relay) so that the driver is not starved by the work behind it, and watch `LinkHandle::quic_stats`; a bounded queue upstream would end it.
36. **A datagram limit names the first hop only (§14.10, §18.5).** The node sizes a frame to the smallest live limit of the sinks of **its own forwarder's** route. When the next hop (a relay's link to a receiver) takes less than the first, a frame between the two is refused at the relay for that receiver, counted there as `sink_error`, and the sender is not told: a `Latest` message too large for the second hop is lost to that receiver at every emission, and the frames of other receivers are not affected. Before the pass every datagram frame of a forwarder-attached node was at most 1,200 octets, whatever the hops took. **Why accepted:** telling the sender what lies beyond the first hop is a wire change (§18.5), and a relay's operator knows the second hop: `attach_forwarder_with(n)` is a ceiling over every channel of the node. **A sink cannot lower the limit of the others below 1,150** (`SINK_LIMIT_FLOOR`; a finding of the review, §18.9): a sink that declares less, such as a QUIC peer that chooses a tiny datagram size, refuses its own copy of larger frames, counted at the side table's `sink_error`, and no more; above the floor a sink's limit takes the frames of the whole route down to it, which is a limit its path has.
37. **A sink that stays full costs the reader of its source face a yield for every frame it refuses (§14.4).** The reader yields once after a side frame a sink refused (`c150061c`), so that the link's own task can drain between the frames of a burst; a sink that is full for good, or over its rate, makes it yield on every frame of that route, and the NDN packets and the other sinks' frames of that face wait one trip through the executor's queue each time. **Why accepted:** a yield is not a wait: with nothing else ready it returns at once, and with other tasks ready it gives them their turn, which is what a reader that shares a worker with the links it feeds needs; the fan-out never blocks on a sink and a route has at most 64 sinks. What a source face sends in is not rated (item 21); a source that floods a full sink is slowed by one executor turn a frame, and that is all. **Bound:** one executor turn a refused frame, and no queue grows.
38. **The side PIT's fairness is per face at each hop, not per receiver, and only the breadcrumbs and the `QUERY` rate of a route are reserved (§14.13).** (a) A relay is **one sink face** at the next hop up, with the one share (`pit_route_reserve`) and the one face allowance (256 breadcrumbs, 512 queries a second) every sink face has, whatever number of receivers stand behind it: a hostile receiver behind a relay can use all of that face's allowance at the hop above, and the other receivers behind the same relay then see their `QUERY` dropped there (`pit_face_full`, `pit_route_full` or `rate_refused`) after the relay forwarded it, and retry with a fresh token when their deadline passes. Each hop does keep its own receivers' shares, so one receiver cannot take another's breadcrumbs **at the relay**. (b) The route's `QUERY` rate up to the source (`route_query_rate`) is **shared out per sink face** (Gate26 F2: before, it was one cell for all the sinks of a route, and four sinks sending junk `QUERY` frames, which cannot be authenticated at the forwarder, took all of it and every other asker of the server was dropped `rate_refused` before the server's person bucket was reached; test `sinks_flooding_junk_cannot_starve_another_sink_of_the_route_rate` **failed first**, a fifth sink's one query a second `Dropped(QueryRate)`): each sink has a share of `route_query_rate / sinks_per_route` (32 a second) with a burst of `route_query_burst / sinks_per_route` (8, at least 1), spends it first and then takes from the route's common cell with the other sinks, and every `QUERY` that goes up is charged to the common cell so that the route stays at its rate. So an asker that stays within its share is never dropped because of another sink's flood, and a lone sink still gets the whole route rate. A share is fixed by the limits (a sink that joins or leaves changes no other's) and a new sink starts with its burst spent, so leaving and joining again gains nothing. What stays: a share belongs to a sink **face**, so a member that holds several sink faces holds that many shares (at most `sinks_per_route`, 64, and never more than the route rate in all), and the askers behind one relay face share its one share. (c) `pit_total` (16,384) is shared by every route and face. **Why accepted:** the forwarder is generic and cannot tell a relay face from a leaf, or know how many receivers stand behind a face, and a per-receiver count would need the identity of a receiver, which the table never sees (it keys by face); reserving by face is the finest grain it has. The harm is bounded: one route's queries, for at most the breadcrumbs' 10 s each, no `ANSWER` is misdelivered, nothing is signed or believed on the forwarder's word, and the source's saturation is bounded by the route's rate and the source face's `SideRate`. An operator who runs relays with many receivers raises `pit_per_face`, `query_rate` and `query_burst` for them (`SideConfig::limits`); a finer grain is a wire change (an asker id in the query prefix) that stage 4 can choose.
39. **A route whose only live sink never drains is held back for as long as the sink is stuck (§14.4, §14.10).** A sink that keeps its face and its `Keep`s alive and reads nothing (a full queue, a ring whose head does not move) has no room for the datagram frames of its route, so the sender's node is refused `Full` for them: the messages of that channel stay pending and coalesce (`Latest`; `full` and `replaced` count it in the channel's stats), and nothing is sent to that sink. The sink does not lapse, since it still sends `Keep`, and the node cannot tell a stuck sink from a slow one. **It does not hold back any other channel of the node:** the node's one waiter (`Connection::side_ready`) wakes for room on any route it was refused for and runs every waiting drain, each of which tests its own route by sending, and it is bounded: at most `ROOM_WAIT_MAX` (1 s) it returns whether or not there is room, the drains run again and the stuck route is refused again and counts it. **Why accepted:** a route with a live sink that takes nothing has nowhere to send, and holding the node's message back (counted, newest kept) is the headroom rule applied to a hop whose sink has gone quiet; dropping it would lose the newest message the sink may yet read, and only the sink's own `Keep`s say whether it is still there. **Bound:** one retry a second per stuck route (and 60 looks a second while it waits, §14.10), the channel's own 1 MiB of waiting payload, the route's 64 sinks; `held_back` in `side/list` counts the refusals.
40. **A stuck QUIC sink is bounded by its link's stall bound, not by the sink's lapse (§14.4, §14.9; Gate26 F8).** A sink is kept live by its `Keep`s whether it reads or not, so the 30 s lapse of item 39 does **not** end the first hop's wait for a peer that keeps its link up and reads nothing: the review found the stream write had no deadline, and `Connection::send_side` for a stream frame waited for as long as that sink was the only live one. A write to the channel stream that takes no octet for `STREAM_STALL` (30 s; `LinkHandle::set_stream_stall` changes it for one link) now closes the link with `CloseReason::Protocol` and words that say why and what to do, and the forwarder drops the face and its sinks: the held-back frame is released within about 30 s of the stream filling, and the datagram class follows when the face is dropped. A slow reader is never closed (any octet the peer takes, a flow-control credit, restarts the bound). Test `a_peer_that_never_reads_its_channel_stream_is_closed_within_the_stall_bound` **failed first** (`a send to a peer that never reads ended within the stall bound: Elapsed(())`), and the engine-level `an_emitter_held_back_by_a_sink_that_never_reads_is_released_when_its_link_is_closed` (`the emitter was held back by a sink that never reads for longer than a minute`). **Residual:** the sink of another face kind (a ring whose head does not move, an app that does not read its inbox) is still held back for as long as it stays: those faces have no bound of their own, and the bound is the `Keep`. The emitter that is released goes on with `Ok` (the engine connection cannot tell a route whose sink left from a route not yet installed), and `EngineConnection` has no notion of a channel's own end (`tx.end`), so a blocked emitter ends when its sink does, not at once.
41. **A second link that merely tries a frame an interval makes a lone grantee a contender (Gate26 F7; ndn-link.md §9 item 9).** `active` counts a link that tries a channel frame in the last interval, however small, so one extra authenticated link that tries one octet a second turns a lone member's rate into `max(capacity, carried) / 2`, and `carried` decays 0.9 an interval to `capacity`: within about a minute the lone member is at `capacity / 2` (64 MiB/s by default) per direction while the other link takes nothing, and N such links push it toward the floor. Pool pinning is the same: any second active link in a window clamps `measured` to the pool, so `carried` cannot rise. **Why accepted:** the count must include refused tries, or a member returning from idle would pass with no bucket (a free burst); a count by octets carried gives a toggle an attacker controls per interval. **Bound:** a link costs an authenticated handshake (`max_links` 1,024), the shares are per link, and the bound is the old fixed `capacity` cap shared by two; no octet beyond the pool is passed, and the owner's own links are never pool members. **What would change it:** max-min fairness on each link's measured demand (a link that uses less than its share lends the slack), a redesign of §9 item 9, with an optional operator `ceiling` if wanted. A grantee cannot raise `carried` with traffic it did not carry: only bytes that passed while it was alone raise it.
42. **A stream frame is held back only at a face with a stream lane, per link stream (§14.4).** The receiving side's back pressure needs the face to read its channel stream apart from its NDN packets, and only the QUIC link does. The IPC socket face (and any transport that mixes a stream-class frame into `recv_bytes`) still loses a stream frame that finds a sink full, counted `sink_full`; holding it would hold that face's NDN packets back too. **Head-of-line within one link's stream:** a sink of one route that does not drain holds back every other route's stream frames of the same link (up to `STREAM_HOLD_MAX`, 10 s), and, at the first sink without room, the other sinks of its own route; the frames of one stream cannot be reordered, and a fan-out that gave the sinks with room the frame first would deliver it twice or out of order after the hold. **After the bound** the stalled sink loses the frame (`stream_stalled`, `sink_full`) and is **demoted** (`stream_demoted`; the log names the face): for a penalty of `STREAM_LOSSY_BASE` (60 s), doubling with each repeat of the same face to an hour and starting again after an hour clean, the lane does not wait for that face, so the link's stream flows for every other sink, and the face gets the stream frames it has room for and loses the rest, counted `sink_full`. A member that reads one frame in a bound therefore holds a link's stream for one bound in each `STREAM_LOSSY_BASE + STREAM_HOLD_MAX`, not for every frame (lane security review F1). A `Reliable` receiver behind a stalled or demoted sink sees a gap: it is told `Lagged { missed }` in place and goes on (§12.6); `Reliable` has no recovery, `Window` asks again from the first missing sequence, and the sender, whose `emit` returned `Ok`, is told nothing. **The bound is a third of the peer's `STREAM_STALL`** so that the lane's release, and the drain of the inbox it held full, come well before the peer's writer finds the link without progress and closes it (lane security review F2; the test `the_lanes_wait_for_a_sink_ends_well_before_the_peers_write_stall` pins the relation between the two constants). A rate-limited sink (`SideRate`, §14.8) is not waited for: a stream frame over its rate is dropped `quota`, as the first hop's hold-back also leaves the rate out.
43. **Room for a stream frame is a hint (lane security review F3).** The lane looks at each live sink with `side_room_for`, then calls `on_frame`; room is not reserved. Two sources whose frames fit the one slot a sink has left both see room and one loses its frame, counted `sink_full` (and the receiver's `Reliable` is told `Lagged`). The claim that a stream frame is not dropped for want of room holds for one producer per sink; a second granted writer routed to the same sink can raise the loss for the others, probabilistically. **Why accepted:** a lane that retried the sinks that refused would offer a frame to a face again after its refusal, and the faces count each refusal (`side_dropped`, `sink_full`, `dropped_over_quota`) as an event, so a retry would count a frame that was then delivered as lost; a reserving room call is a change to every face kind. The loss is visible at the sink's forwarder, in the receiver's notice, and the writer that causes it is a member.
44. **The demotion is per face, and colluding or re-dialling members add up (lane security review F1).** The penalty of item 42 is kept in the table's entry of the sink face (it survives the sink lapsing and the face going down and being dialled at the same id, and is dropped an hour after it ended). **K** members whose links are sinks of routes of one source link, each holding the stream for one bound in turn, hold it for K bounds in each `STREAM_LOSSY_BASE + STREAM_HOLD_MAX`; a member that dials a new link gets a face with no history, so its penalty starts again at `STREAM_LOSSY_BASE`. The cost of each is a granted identity and a link admission (ndn-link.md §7), which is the link layer's bound and not this table's. The head-of-line rule is a property of one ordered stream; the fix is not to wait, which is a loss.
45. **A lane drains what a link received before it ended; a faulted link's stream is not faulted (lane security review, info).** When a `Permanent` face's connection dies its reader ends without removing the face, and its lane then takes the frames the link had already received (at most 1,024, 4 MiB) and routes them under the same hold rule; they were received from the peer before the link ended, and each is judged as any frame is. `ndn-link`'s `Faulted` wrapper passes the stream through unchanged (`recv_side_stream`): the stream is ordered and lossless by QUIC's guarantee, so a fault plan that dropped its frames would model nothing a link does; the datagram frames and NDN packets keep the plan.

## 14. The forwarder's side table

A forwarder that carries channels holds one table for their frames, the **side table**. It copies each frame to the faces that asked for it, once per face, and knows nothing of what a frame is. This section says what the table keeps (§14.1, §14.2), how a route comes to be (§14.3), how a frame and a keep-alive go through it (§14.4, §14.5), how a route is kept and ends (§14.6), how a face is barred (§14.7) and paced (§14.8), what a face has to carry (§14.9), what a node does to use the table (§14.10), the management commands and counters (§14.11), the threat notes (§14.12) and how a request goes up and its reply comes back to one face (the side PIT, §14.13).

Implementation: `ndn-packet` (`lp/`: the `SideRoute` header), `ndn-transport` (`Transport::forward_side_from`, `side_header.rs`), `ndn-engine` (`side.rs`: the table; `dispatcher/inbound.rs`: the peel; `dispatcher/outbound.rs`: the install), `ndn-mgmt` (`modules/side.rs`), `ndn-face-local` (the app face's side inbox), `ndn-app` (`Connection`), `ndn-face` (`l2/`: the Ethernet faces), `ndn-heap` (`chan/route.rs`, `chan/bar.rs`: the node's part) and, in ndn-ext, `ndn-link` (`FaceSink`, [ndn-link.md](ndn-link.md) §9).

### 14.1 The table

`SideTable` is owned by the `ForwarderEngine` (`ForwarderEngine::side()`; `EngineConfig::side` sets it). It is **generic**: it holds opaque 64-bit ids, `FaceId`s, times and counters, and nothing else. It never sees a name, a key, a space, a person or a mode; it decrypts and verifies nothing; the frames it reads beyond the routing header are `UP` (§11.5), its own keep-alive, and the tokens of `QUERY` and `ANSWER` (§14.13). It is not the FIB and not the PIT: routes come and go with sessions, and nothing in the table is a name.

A **route** has an id (the channel id, which the table treats as 64 opaque bits), **one source face**, one to 64 **sink faces**, and counters of the frames and octets it routed. A **sink** is a face, an expiry and the time its last `Keep` was passed upstream. A face may be the source of some routes and a sink of others. The source is the face the setup Data arrived on, the next hop toward the sender; a sink is a face whose Interest that Data answered, a hop toward a receiver.

### 14.2 Limits and refusals

The bounds are `SideLimits`, set by a host in `EngineConfig::side` (`SideConfig`).

| What | Default | Over it |
|---|---|---|
| Routes in the table | 4,096 | the install is refused `TableFull` |
| Routes one source face feeds | 256 | `SourceFull` |
| Sinks on one route | 64 | `SinkCap` |
| Routes one face is a sink of | 1,024 | `SinkFaceFull` |
| TTL of a sink | the header's `ttl_secs`; 0 is 30 s (`ttl_default`) | a TTL over 300 s (`ttl_max`) is clamped to it |
| `Keep`s passed upstream | a sink passes 8 in quick succession (`keep_forward_burst`: the first `Keep` of each receiver behind one link), then one every 5 s (`keep_forward_min`) | the `Keep` refreshes the sink and goes no further |
| Sweep of lapsed sinks | every 1 s, on the engine's clock | |
| Bar | 60 s, doubling to 1 h (§14.7) | |
| Rate of a sink face | 1 MB/s on radio kinds, off on Ethernet kinds (§14.8) | the frame is dropped for that sink, `quota` |
| The app face's side inbox | 256 frames and 8 MiB | `Full`, counted |
| Breadcrumbs of the side PIT, `QUERY` rate, life of a breadcrumb | 16,384 in all, 256 a face, 1,024 a route with 8 held for each live sink; 512 a second a face, burst 128, and 2,048 a second a route, burst 512; 10 s (`pit_total`, `pit_per_face`, `pit_per_route`, `pit_route_reserve`, `query_rate`, `query_burst`, `route_query_rate`, `route_query_burst`, `pit_ttl_max`) | the `QUERY` is dropped and counted (§14.13) |

`SideTable::install` refuses with a `SideRefusal`, leaves the table as it was, and each refusal is counted (`refused_*`, §14.11):

| Refusal (`label`) | What it says |
|---|---|
| `TableFull` (`table_full`) | the table holds its most routes |
| `SourceFull` (`source_full`) | the upstream face already feeds its most routes |
| `SinkCap` (`sink_cap`) | the route already fans out to 64 faces |
| `SinkFaceFull` (`sink_face_full`) | the receiver's face is already a sink of its most routes |
| `SourceMismatch` (`source_mismatch`) | a route with this id exists with another source: the id is in use or the path changed; wait for the old route to lapse |
| `SinkIsSource` (`sink_is_source`) | the receiver's face is the face the Data arrived on: a route cannot loop back |
| `Quarantined` (`quarantined`) | the upstream face is barred (§14.7) |
| `FaceGone` (`face_gone`) | a face of the route is no longer on this forwarder |
| `NotCarried` (`not_carried`) | a face of the route cannot carry channel frames (`side_frame_max` is 0 or it carries no datagram class: a TCP, UDP or WebSocket face): a route over it could never carry a frame or a `Keep`; the receiver is reached over a link that does, or through a relay |
| `NotAsked` (`not_asked`) | the Data did not come from a face its Interest was sent out of: a route is installed only from the face that answered the request |

The message of each names what to look at and what to raise or end.

### 14.3 How a route is made

**The label.** A route is made by the session's own Data. The Data carries an NDNLPv2 header, `SideRoute` (`LP_SIDE_ROUTE`, TLV-TYPE **0x0370**; `ndn_packet::lp::SideRoute { id, ttl_secs }` in `LpHeaders::side_route`). Its value is exactly 10 octets: the route id (8, big-endian) then the TTL in seconds (2, big-endian). A value of any other length makes the whole LpPacket a decode error (fail closed). The type is even, so a decoder that predates it skips it (last paragraph of this section). It follows the NDNLPv2 header order: after every header of a lower type (`CachePolicy` 0x0334, `CongestionMark` 0x0340, the A-LAL fields 0x0360 to 0x0364) and before the Fragment; one out of order or twice is a decode error, as for any LP header. On a fragmented Data it counts only on the final fragment, the one the reassembled packet continues as. The registry is the NDNLPv2 table of [README.md](README.md#ndnlpv2-header-numbers). The wire of a header with id `1122334455667788` and TTL `0x99AA` (39,338 s; a forwarder clamps it to 300 s) is:

```
fd03700a112233445566778899aa
```

**The install.** The Data pipeline installs routes in the step that sends a matched Data on, after the PIT matched and, where the engine validates Data, after the Data validated. For each face `f` in the PIT entry's in-records other than the face `a` the Data arrived on, it calls `install_live(id, a, f, ttl, faces)` and sends the Data to `f` **with the header again**, carrying the TTL the table kept (the header's, 0 becoming 30 s and more than 300 s becoming 300 s). The next forwarder does the same. The install is made only if (1) the Interest went **out of `a`**, that is, an out-record of the PIT entry the Data consumed names `a` (the PIT stage marks a Data that no out-record accounts for, and every `f` is then refused `NotAsked`: the Data is forwarded as ever, only the route is refused); (2) both faces are **on this forwarder**, which `install_live` reads under the table's own lock (the teardown of a face leaves the face table before `remove_face` takes that lock, §14.6, so an install either sees the face gone, `FaceGone`, or has its sink removed by the teardown that follows); and (3) both faces **carry channel frames**: `side_frame_max` is not 0 and the datagram class is carried (`NotCarried` otherwise, §14.9). The header is echoed to `f` only with an installed route, so a face that cannot carry channel frames (a TCP or WebSocket face, a bare-TLV IPC socket) never gets a header, a sink or a route, and its Data is the Data an NDN consumer expects (bare TLV on a face that uses none). A Data that carries the header goes to a face that carries side frames in an LpPacket even where the face does not use LP framing (as one with a PitToken does). Installing a sink the route already has refreshes its TTL; another face adds a sink.

**A refusal** (§14.2) is counted and logged at `warn` with the route, the two faces and the reason, and that face gets the Data **without** the header: it still gets the session and gets no frames, so its receiver reports `NoPath` after 2 s (§14.10).

**When nothing is installed.** A Data served from the Content Store carries no header (the session Data is `NoCache`, so none is); a Data that matched no PIT entry has no in-record, so no sink; a Data from a face no Interest of this forwarder went out of installs nothing (`NotAsked`: any station on a shared medium, or a hop that does not validate, can send a Data whose name is pending, and its arrival face is then no source); a face that cannot carry channel frames is neither sink nor source (`NotCarried`); and the arrival face is never offered.

**`NoCache` goes on.** A forwarder that receives a Data with `CachePolicy = NoCache` does not store it and sends it on with `NoCache` again, on every face (a bare IPC face too, which is framed whenever a header must go), so that no forwarder past the first hop caches a session Data either. A cache hit has no header of the packet's own to pass on.

**Where the faces are.** At the sender's own forwarder the Data arrives from the sender's app face, which becomes the source. At the receiver's forwarder it arrives from a link and the receiver's app face is the sink. At a relay both are links. A route therefore runs from the sender's app face through every hop to the app face of every receiver, and exists only where a receiver's Interest went.

**Why a stray or forged header does little.**

- A route is installed only for a Data that matched a PIT entry, so a face becomes a sink only if it **asked**: its Interest is the in-record.
- The source is the face the Data arrived on, and only if an Interest of this forwarder went out of that face (`NotAsked` otherwise); it is therefore a face an Interest to the sender went out of. A frame of the id from any other face is dropped (§14.4).
- The table counts everything (§14.2). A second Data that names an id already routed from another source is refused `SourceMismatch`. The ids are 64 random bits (§3.2) and a forwarder holds at most 4,096 routes, so two live routes collide by chance with probability about 2^-41.
- What a hostile next hop can do is send frames into the routes it is the source of. They fail the AEAD at every receiver or, from a key holder, fall to the checkpoints (§15).
- None of this rests on validation: a forwarder that validates no Data (a relay in transit, ndn-link.md §15.7, or a profile with security off) installs on the PIT match alone.

The label is **not** covered by the Data's signature: it is a claim of the hop that sent the Data on. A hostile next hop is on the path anyway; with the label it can also name an id that is not the channel's (the receiver then reports `NoPath`), or the id of a channel not yet routed here (that channel's own session Data is then refused `SourceMismatch` here until the squatted route lapses, at most 300 s without `Keep`s). The receiver's judgement of the Data (§3.4) is no judgement of the label.

**A forwarder that predates the header.** It skips the even type, forwards the Data without it and installs nothing. No frame crosses it, and the receiver reports `NoPath` with the next step "a forwarder between you and the sender does not carry channel frames: update it" (the text adds the other cause it cannot tell apart, a lost or delayed first `Keep`: ask again, §13.12 item 27). The setup has no other path to fall back to: it **fails closed**.

### 14.4 The data path

**The peel.** The reader of every face looks at the first octet of what it read. If it is `0x00` the frame is a channel frame: the reader adds it to the face's `in_side_frames` and `in_side_bytes`, stamps the face's activity (§14.6), calls `SideTable::on_frame(face, frame, faces)` and reads on. That is one comparison, made before NDNLPv2 detection and before the pipeline's bounded channel, so a channel frame never costs a PIT entry, a task, an `in_drops` count or a malformed-packet verdict, and an NDN packet never takes this path (no NDN packet starts with `0x00`, [ndn-link.md](ndn-link.md) §9 item 7). A frame the table does not route is dropped there, counted.

**The receiving side of a stream: the lane waits, the reader does not (back pressure).** A face whose transport reads its **channel stream apart** from the rest (`Transport::side_stream_lane()`; the QUIC link, whose one unidirectional stream is not mixed with its datagrams) has that stream's frames read by a task of its own, the **stream lane** (`run_side_stream_lane`; the transport's `recv_side_stream`). The lane counts each frame like the reader (`in_side_frames`, `in_side_bytes`), and **before `on_frame` it waits until every live sink of the frame's route that it waits for has room for it** (`SideTable::stream_room`: for a frame of a route whose source is this face and not barred, each live sink that is still a face and not demoted says `side_room_for(frame, class)`; an `UP`, a `QUERY`, an `ANSWER`, a frame of no route or of another source goes at once to `on_frame`, which counts it as ever). While it waits it takes nothing more from the stream, so the transport stops reading the peer's octets (the link's inbox of 4 MiB fills, its stream task stops reading the connection), QUIC flow control slows the peer, and the first-hop hold-back of the sender's own forwarder (§14.4, above) holds the sender's frames in hand: the back pressure runs from the app's inbox through the link to the emitter, and **a stream frame is not dropped for want of room at a sink that drains** (`sink_full` stays 0 for it). The lane looks by yielding, then sleeping 1, 2, 4, 8, 16 ms between looks, as the first hop's wait does. `stream_held` counts a frame that had to wait, `stream_stalled` one that waited the bound. **The rule is per link stream, head-of-line:** the frames of one stream stay in order, so one sink without room holds back the frames behind its frame for every route of that stream (and the other sinks of its own route, which get a frame only when all have room); sinks of other links, and every datagram, are untouched. **The bound is `STREAM_HOLD_MAX` (10 s, a third of the link's own `STREAM_STALL`):** a sink still without room after it is dead for the stream, and the frame is routed anyway (the sinks with room take it, the full one loses it, counted `sink_full` and `stream_stalled`), and **each such sink is demoted** for `STREAM_LOSSY_BASE` (60 s, doubling per repeat to an hour; `stream_demoted`, a `warn` log naming the source and the sink face): the lane does not wait for a demoted sink, so one sink holds the stream once and not for every frame (§13.12 items 42 and 44). Before the bound a peer that keeps writing finds its flow-control window full; the lane's release comes before the peer's `STREAM_STALL` (30 s) without progress would close its link (§14.9, item 40 of §13.12). **NDN traffic is never stalled by it**: the lane is a task of its own, and the face's reader keeps reading its NDN packets and datagram frames (a datagram frame that finds a sink full is still dropped, counted `sink_full`, as designed: `Latest` loses nothing it did not choose to lose). A face that does not read its stream apart (the default: a stream class frame arrives through `recv_bytes` beside the NDN packets, as on the IPC socket face, §14.9) cannot be held back without holding its NDN packets back, so its stream frame that finds a sink full is dropped and counted `sink_full`, as before (§13.12 item 42).

**A locally originated frame** goes to the table from the node's own face, and does not wait in the face's queue (`Connection::send_side` for a stream frame, `try_send_side` for a datagram, §14.10). The node's connection to an embedded engine (`EngineConnection`, `ForwarderEngine::side_send_local`) hands it to **`SideTable::send_local(from, frame, faces)`** at once, and that is `on_frame` with one refusal in front: a data-class frame (`DATA`, `CHECKPOINT`, `CONTROL`) **of either class** of a route whose source is `from` (not barred), whose live sinks **all** have no room for **this frame** now (`Face::side_room_for`, below), is **refused `Full` to the caller**, counted `held_back` and nothing else: it is not lost, the node keeps the message pending (a datagram) or the frame in hand (a stream frame) and offers it again when `SideTable::room(&frame)` (`ForwarderEngine::side_room`) says a sink has room for the frame it was refused. A route with no live sink has nothing to wait for (`room` is true) and the frame goes to `on_frame`, which drops it as before. Any other frame (an `UP`, a `QUERY`, an `ANSWER`, one for no route, from a barred face, from a face that is not the route's source) is `on_frame`'s, with its own counts, and is `Ok`. The face's `in_side_frames` and `in_side_bytes` count what `send_local` took, as the reader counts what it peels. `send_local` never blocks. (Before this change a stream frame went into the face's queue and the reader peeled it: a `Reliable` or `Window` frame that the link's stream queue or quota had no room for was dropped there, counted `sink_full`, and its sender told `Ok`; the receiver saw a gap that the sender's own node had made.)

**`on_frame`** never blocks, so the reader goes on reading the face's NDN packets whatever a sink does with the frame. It does, in this order, and a frame that fails a step costs nothing below it:

1. Parse the 10-octet routing header and, for an `UP`, its 11-octet record: `malformed`.
2. `from` is barred (§14.7) and the frame is not an `UP`: `quarantined`. (An `UP` is a face's own sink management and passes the bar, §14.5.)
3. Look the id up (a map read): no route, `unrouted`.
4. An `UP`: §14.5.
5. Any other kind: `from` must be the route's source, else `wrong_source`. Then fan out.

**Fan-out.** For each sink:

- A sink whose expiry has passed is skipped, even before the sweep removes it.
- A sink face with a rate (§14.8) whose bucket is empty is skipped: `quota`.
- A sink face that is no longer on the forwarder: `sink_full`.
- Otherwise the table calls `Face::forward_side_from(frame.clone(), class, from)`, `class` being the header's class bit. `Bytes::clone` is a reference count: the frame is **not copied per sink**. A taken frame counts in `frames_out`; the result `Full` counts `sink_full`, `Unsupported`, `NotCarried` and `PeerLacksChannels` count `class_unsupported`, and any other refusal counts `sink_error`.

**`forward_side_from(frame, class, ingress)`** is the non-blocking hand-over of a frame to a transport: `Transport`, `ErasedTransport` and `Face` all have it. `ingress` is the face the frame arrived on at this forwarder. The default ignores it and calls `forward_side` (itself `Unsupported` by default); a transport that delivers into an application keeps it, so that the application can say which link a frame came over (§14.9). Each also has **`side_carries(class)`**: whether the transport's medium carries side frames of that class, a fact of the medium and not of the queue's room. The default is whether `side_frame_max` is nonzero (a transport that opted in takes both classes); a transport with no ordered, reliable stream says `false` for the stream class (the Ethernet faces, and the writer of the ring carrier, §14.9), and the default `send_side` refuses a class the transport does not carry `NotCarried`. The engine exposes it as `ForwarderEngine::face_carries_side(face, class)` (`false` for a face it does not hold) and learns nothing of what the frames mean.

**`side_room(class)`** is the read `forward_side_from` makes first, without the frame: whether a frame of that class handed over now would be taken and not refused `Full` (`Transport`, `ErasedTransport` and `Face` have it). **`side_room_for(frame, class)`** is the same read for a frame in hand: for a face that sends a frame on only some of its queues (a radio whose policy picks the phys by frame) it answers for the queues that frame goes to, and its default is `side_room(class)`. Both are **hints**: room is never reserved, and a frame can still find the queue full by the time it is sent (then the sink loses it and counts `sink_full`, as on any hop). They never block. They are not exact for every face either: the hint can say room for a frame the face then refuses `Full`, because the read does not count the frame's own octets (a queue under its bound by less than the frame) or a quota the send checks later; the frame is then lost at that sink and counted `sink_full`, as it was before the hold-back, which narrows that loss and does not close it. The QUIC link's `side_room_for` counts the stream frame's octets against the stream queue's bound and the quota's bucket for the frame of either class (§14.9). The default is `true`: a transport with no queue to fill has nothing to wait for, and one that does not implement it is never held back for (it loses the frame as before). §14.9 says which faces implement it.

**A sink that cannot take a frame loses it, visibly.** The relay drops it for that sink, counts it (`sink_full`, `quota`, `class_unsupported`, `sink_error`; `side/list` shows them) and goes on. A receiver behind it sees a gap in `Reliable` and `Window` (§12.6); in `Latest` nothing is noticed (§12.1). A relay is lossy under congestion, and says so. **The first hop is the exception** for a data frame, of either class, that the sender's own node hands over (`send_local`, above): when no sink has room it is refused `Full` to the node and counted `held_back`, not `sink_full`, since the node still has it. A sink that had room at the look and refused all the same costs the frame, counted `sink_full`.

**The receiving side's exception for the stream class.** The paragraph above is what a relay does with a datagram frame, and with a stream frame at a face that has no stream lane. A stream frame that arrives on a stream lane (the QUIC link) is **not dropped for want of room at a sink the lane waits for**: the lane waits, as the paragraph on the peel above says, and `sink_full` counts a stream frame only when a sink that had room at the look refused it all the same (another sender filled it in between, §13.12 item 43), when a sink face was gone from the face table, at a demoted sink (§13.12 item 42), or after `STREAM_HOLD_MAX`.

**A held-back stream frame waits as long as a sink is live and its link takes octets, and no longer.** `Connection::send_side` for a stream frame waits while `side_room_for(frame)` says no. The wait ends when the route has no live sink (its link closed and the forwarder dropped the face, the sink lapsed, or the route was removed) or the connection's own face is gone (`Closed`). A sink is kept live by its `Keep`s whether it reads or not, so the 30 s lapse does not end the wait for a peer that keeps its link up and reads nothing; **the link's stall bound does** (§14.9, §13.12 item 40): a QUIC link whose channel stream takes no octet for `STREAM_STALL` (30 s) is closed, and the sink goes with the face. At a forwarder behind it the same sink holds the receiving lane for at most `STREAM_HOLD_MAX` once, and is then demoted (§14.4, §13.12 item 42).

### 14.5 `UP`: keep and unsub

A face sends an `UP` frame (§11.5) to say something about a route it is a sink of. The table reads it as its own, answers nothing, and changes only that face's own sink.

- **`Keep`** from a sink refreshes **that sink's** expiry to its TTL from now (§14.6). The table passes the frame to the route's source face as a datagram (its octets unchanged, the sink's face as the ingress) and notes it, while that sink is within its **burst**: it may pass `keep_forward_burst` (8) `Keep`s in quick succession, and after that one more for every 5 s (`keep_forward_min`) that went by (a cell-rate limiter: each passed `Keep` is paid for with 5 s, and one passes while at most seven are still owed). A `Keep` the source could not take is not noted, so the next one tries again. A sink's first `Keep` goes up at once at every hop, so the sender learns that a receiver's route is up. **Why a burst and not one at once:** at the hop before a relay, the relay's one link is a single sink for every receiver behind it, so the second receiver's first `Keep` is not that sink's first. Held 5 s, it would leave the sender's stream gate (§14.10) waiting for it until `ROUTE_WAIT` ran out, a second in which the sender's checkpoints and binds stand still and a `Latest` receiver, holding frames a checkpoint has not vouched for, drops them. The burst lets the first `Keep` of eight receivers behind one link through at once; the rate over a longer time is what it was, one frame per sink every 5 s at each hop. A ninth receiver that joins within the seconds the burst takes to pay back waits for the sender's gate as an ask whose `Keep` was lost does (§13.12 item 26).
- **`Unsub`** from a sink removes that face's sink. If the route has no sink left it is removed and the `Unsub` goes on to the source; otherwise nothing goes up.
- An `UP` from a face that is not a sink of the route is dropped (`wrong_source`), barred or not: a bar (§14.7) does not stop a face's own `Keep` and `Unsub` (they are bounded as any are), so the routes it receives over stay up and the senders upstream go on sending to it; one for an unknown id, `unrouted`; one that does not parse, `malformed`.

### 14.6 Lifetime and teardown

- **TTL.** A sink lapses its TTL after its last install or `Keep`. The 1 s sweep removes lapsed sinks and a route left with none, and counts `sinks_lapsed`. A receiver that left or was removed stops sending `Keep`, so its entries lapse at every hop within the TTL. A removed member that goes on sending `Keep` holds a sink and is sent ciphertext it cannot open: the forwarder judges no membership (§14.12).
- **The session Data asks for 30 s** (`SIDE_TTL`); a node sends a `Keep` for each session it holds every 10 s (`KEEP_EVERY`, a third of the TTL, so two lost ones still leave the sink alive) and an `Unsub` when it lets a session go (§14.10).
- **A face that goes down takes its routes with it.** `SideTable::remove_face(face)` drops every route the face is the source of and every sink on it, and a route left with no sink. It is called wherever the engine removes a face, **after** the face has left the face table (§14.3): `ForwarderEngine::remove_face` and `EngineDiscoveryContext::remove_face`, the tail of the face's reader (the persistent branch too), the idle reaper, the close after a send error and `faces/destroy` of a face with no token. The 1 s sweep also drops every route whose source or sink face is no longer in the face table, as a catch for any other exit. The bar of the face and its history stay (§14.7) and its rate goes.
- **A link that loses an identity and stays.** When a link loses an identity but another membership still admits it, `Links` calls `FaceSink::side_remove_face(face)` for each of its faces: channel authority is link-wide and a route's id cannot be tied to a namespace, so any identity lost counts, which is the fail-closed reading. No frame then crosses the link on the strength of the lost identity; a receiver behind it asks again and the new Data installs what the remaining identities allow ([ndn-link.md](ndn-link.md) §9 item 8).
- **Activity.** The quiet-close rule counts a face with a side route as routed and counts the octets of channel frames on its stream as activity only while the forwarder holds such a route ([ndn-link.md](ndn-link.md) §16.2). The engine's own idle reaper, which removes a connectionless on-demand face (UDP and the like) after 5 minutes without traffic, counts a channel frame as traffic as it counts an NDN packet: a frame **received** on the face (stamped by its reader), a frame **handed to it as a sink** and an `UP` record passed to it as a route's source (stamped by the side table). A face that carries only channel frames is not reaped, and its routes stay; one that carries nothing is still reaped, with its routes.
- **No route, no send.** `SideTable::sinks(id)` is the number of live sinks. A sender whose route has none sends nothing that reaches anyone (§14.10).

### 14.7 The bar

A face is **barred** when a frame that came over it is shown to be a forgery (§15.5, §15.6). A bar is on what the face **sources**: the channel frames it sends to this forwarder for the routes it is the source of. NDN traffic over the face goes on, the forwarder still sends the face the frames of routes it is a sink of, and the face's own sink management (`Keep`, `Unsub`) still counts: a bar does not cut the link in both directions.

- **What a barred face loses.** The frames it sends as a route's source are dropped (`quarantined`), and no route is installed with it as the source (`Quarantined`). Its `UP` records, `Keep` and `Unsub`, are **not** dropped: they are its own sinks' management, bounded as any are and `WrongSource` unless the face is a sink of the route. Dropping them would let every sink the face holds lapse within the TTL and end the senders' routes to it, so the bar would cut what the forwarder sends it as well; and a barred relay's receivers, who did not forge anything, would lose their routes with it.
- **`bar(face, now)`** applies the next length of the face's history: 60 s, doubling for each repeat on the same face (120 s, 240 s, …) to 1 h, and 60 s again once the face has gone an hour after its last bar ended without another. It returns the length applied. **While a bar is live a call changes nothing**, history included: one incident reported twice (a checkpoint lags the frames) is one bar and not two doublings, and the call returns what is left of the live bar. The next call after the bar ended doubles.
- **`quarantine_for(face, now, length)`** bars for a length from now and keeps a longer bar the face already has; the length is capped at 1 h (a length of `u64::MAX` milliseconds is an hour, never a bar that does not end) and the call returns the length applied; **`lift(face)`** ends the bar now and keeps the history, so the next `bar` still doubles. Times are nanoseconds on the table's clock (`SideTable::now()`, §14.12).
- **History.** A face's bar and its history are kept per face id and survive `remove_face`, so a face dialled again at the same id is still barred; the sweep forgets a history an hour after its last bar ended.
- **Who bars.** A node that has evidence (§15.5) asks its forwarder through its `IngressBar` (`NodeHandle::attach_bar`; `attach_forwarder` attaches one over the node's connection, `Connection::side_quarantine`). An embedded engine's connection calls `bar(face, now)` and refuses its own app face, since barring that would cut the app off from its forwarder and no link delivered the frame, and refuses a face the engine does not hold (the id is the host's to choose, a bar is state per face, and a refusal creates none). An operator or an out-of-process host uses the signed management command `side/quarantine` (§14.11). **What is said of the outcome:** the alert is raised at once and says the face was asked to be barred, not that it was; with no `IngressBar` attached the node logs that the face is `NOT barred` and what to do, and a connection's bar logs `barred for N s` or `could NOT be barred` with the forwarder's reason (§13.12 item 24).
- **A bar of a shared segment.** A multicast Ethernet face is one face for every station on the segment (§14.9), so a bar of it is a bar of **all** of them: every station's channel frames over that face are dropped for the bar's length, and a repeat doubles it for all. A station that makes a receiver see a contradicted frame there bars its neighbours too (§13.12 item 20).
- **The bar is local.** It is made at the forwarder of the node that holds the evidence. Nothing carries it to another forwarder, upstream or beside.

### 14.8 The rate of a face

`SideRate { bytes_per_sec }` is a token bucket on what the table hands one **sink face**, with one second of burst. A frame is admitted while the bucket holds tokens, whatever its size, and charged its length (the bucket may go negative, so a large frame is not refused for ever at a low rate); over it the frame is dropped for that sink and counted `quota`. `UP` records passed upstream are not charged.

The default is 1 MB/s on the radio kinds (`FaceKind::Wfb`, `WifiAware`, `WifiDirect`), so a channel cannot fill a radio, and off on the Ethernet kinds (`Ethernet`, `EtherMulticast`) and on every other kind. `SideConfig::radio_rate` and `ethernet_rate` set it per kind, `SideTable::set_rate(face, rate)` per face; a face gets its kind's rate when it is added. A radio face also has its own bound below the table, the phy's side lane (§14.9), which refuses `Full` at once; the rate is the table's, the lane's bound is the radio's, and neither waits.

### 14.9 What a face carries

A face carries channel frames only if it opts in (§11: `side_frame_max()` above 0) and answers `forward_side_from` without waiting.

**Room (`side_room`, `side_room_for`).** Whether a face has room for a channel frame is what holds a node's data frame back at the first hop. `side_room(class)` reads it without a frame; **`side_room_for(frame, class)`** (§14.4) reads it for the frame the sender holds, and is what the side table asks. The default of `side_room_for` is `side_room(class)`, the answer for a face whose bound is in frames; **a face that bounds a queue in octets MUST override it** with the test its `forward_side_from` makes, since a default that reads only "the queue is under its bound" says room for a frame the face then refuses `Full`, and the frame is lost at the first hop that held it back for its sender (Gate26 F9). The faces that answer it: the **in-process app face** (the inbox holds **this frame** under its 8 MiB byte bound, and a frame slot is free); **QUIC link faces** (the datagram class: fewer octets queued than the headroom; the stream class: the stream queue holds this frame under `STREAM_QUEUE_MAX`; and, where the link has a quota, **its bucket holds the frame** (`FairMember::would_admit`, a read that spends nothing and marks no activity, and that divides the pool as `admit` will at the same instant, the window roll included; a member alone always has room), so a frame `forward_side` would refuse `Full` for the quota is held back for its sender and not sent to be dropped; not the datagram's own octets against the headroom); **stream faces with a side lane**, i.e. the IPC socket face (the lane holds this frame and its 13-octet record header under both its frame and octet bounds, the test `forward_side` makes); **Ethernet faces** (a free slot in the port's send ring, the datagram class; `EtherPort::has_tx_room`); **shared-memory `SpscFace`** (a free slot in the ring: a slot takes any frame that fits it; a frame the face refuses another way, an NDN packet or one over a slot, is not waited for); the **radio faces** (each phy's `SideLane` under its bounds). A `Radio` picks the phys by the frame, so its `side_room_for` judges **the lanes of the phys the policy picks for that frame**, each with room for the frame's octets (`SideLane::has_room_for`): a lane the frame does not go to, however full or however long its phy has not drained, holds nothing back, and a frame that `forward_side` would refuse for another reason (no phy picked, a phy that cannot carry it, over the MTU) is not one that waits for room. Its frame-less `side_room(class)` says yes only when **every** phy has room, the answer for a frame that goes to all of them. The **simulated face** (its far queue has a permit). The ring carrier is never full and says yes; a face with no queue to fill says yes (the default). A link that has ended refuses a stream frame `Closed` and not `Full`, and says room (nothing to wait for).

**A peer that never reads is closed (QUIC link faces; Gate26 F8).** A write to the channel stream that makes no progress for `STREAM_STALL` (30 s; `LinkHandle::set_stream_stall` changes it for one link) closes the link with `CloseReason::Protocol` and the words "your device did not read the channel stream for 30s while this device had channel frames to send it, so this link is closed; read the channel stream (or leave the channels you follow) and link again". Progress is any octet the peer takes, so a slow reader is never closed. The write in flight is refused `Io` with the same words, and the forwarder drops the link's face and sinks.

- **The in-process app face** (`ndn-face-local`). `side_frame_max` is 1 MiB (`STREAM_FRAME_GRANT`: a channel's datagram frame, 9,000 octets, and a shared-memory slot, 8,960, both fit). The engine's `forward_side_from(frame, class, ingress)` pushes `(frame, ingress)` into the face's **side inbox**, apart from the NDN packets (which stay on `recv`), at most 256 frames and 8 MiB. It never waits: a frame that finds either bound is refused `Full` and counted (`InProcHandle::side_dropped`), and its receiver sees the gap as a congested link's. Without an ingress (`forward_side`) the face refuses `Unsupported`. The app's own frames go the other way through the face's reader (`InProcHandle::send`, `try_send`).
- **QUIC link faces** (ndn-ext `ndn-face-quic`, through `ndn-link`): both classes, the datagram by the headroom rule and the stream through a bounded queue; `Full` when there is no room. Which links carry channel frames at all is [ndn-link.md](ndn-link.md) §9 item 8. A grant link's bytes are also held to its namespace's fair share, which is work-conserving: a link alone in its pool takes no bucket (the link's congestion control and the bounds above decide), links that contend each get `max(capacity, carried) / active` bytes a second each way (`ndn-link.md` §9 item 9); a frame over it is refused `Full` by `forward_side` and counted `dropped_over_quota` at the link.
- **A face's stream lane** (`Transport::side_stream_lane`, `recv_side_stream`; §14.4). The QUIC link face reads its channel stream's frames through `recv_side_stream` and **not** through `recv_bytes`: the stream's task reads the connection's stream into an inbox of 4 MiB, `recv_side_stream` takes a frame from it, and not taking one is the back pressure that reaches the peer through QUIC flow control. `recv_bytes` is then NDN packets and datagram frames only. The default is no lane (`false`; `recv_side_stream` never completes), and a transport that wraps another (ndn-link's fault injector) forwards both, since its plan acts on what `recv_bytes` returns.
- **Ethernet faces** (`NamedEtherFace`, `MulticastEtherFace`, ndn-face `l2/`, the Linux `AF_PACKET` faces): the **datagram class only**. `forward_side` pushes into the face's send queue and flushes without waiting: `Full` when the queue has no room, `TooLarge` over the MTU (a frame is never fragmented), `Malformed` for a frame that is not a channel frame, and `NotCarried` for the stream class, so a hop with no stream does not silently carry a reliable channel (the table counts `class_unsupported`). A NIC pads a payload under 46 octets; §11.3 is why no frame is that short. The macOS and Windows Ethernet faces have no `forward_side`. **Each frame leaves for its own destination.** A `SOCK_DGRAM` packet socket takes the destination of every frame of one `sendto` from the call's address (the address inside a TPACKET_V2 TX frame is not read on transmit), so the ring (`af_packet.rs`) keeps the destination each frame was queued with in a preallocated per-slot table and flushes one run of frames of the same destination per `sendto`, with the queue lock held across the call. A unicast reply queued among channel frames to the multicast group, or the reverse, therefore reaches the station it was queued for; a run the kernel did not take keeps its destination and goes first at the next flush, and a queued frame keeps its slot until it is flushed (a push into a full ring is refused, never overwrites). **A unicast face hears its one peer.** On a shared segment any station can address this station's MAC, and a unicast face stands for one named peer, not for the segment: `NamedEtherFace::recv_bytes` hands the forwarder only the frames whose source is the named peer's MAC (as the macOS and Windows variants do) and drops the rest before the forwarder sees them, so a third station cannot speak as that face (send an `UP` for its sinks, send frames of a route it sources, make the forwarder bar it). A `MulticastEtherFace` stands for every station and cannot be filtered so (§13.12 item 20).
- **`MulticastEtherFace` as one sink.** A frame sent to it is **one** Ethernet frame to the NDN multicast group for every station on the segment. A station that is no receiver sees a sealed frame and, holding no route for its id, drops it (`unrouted`). A station that is a receiver holds a route whose source is the face its multicast face is on, learnt from the same session Data (the Data's in-record includes the multicast face). Two receivers behind one multicast face cost the sender's link one frame.
- **Shared memory** (ndn-ext `ndn-face-shm`, `SpscFace`): a frame is one ring slot (`side_frame_max` is the slot size). The ring is reliable and ordered, so both classes are carried; `send_side` waits for a free slot, and `forward_side` is a non-blocking push (`Full` at once when no slot is free, `Malformed` and `TooLarge` as for any face) that wakes a parked reader. The push side of the ring is exclusive (a lock), because the sender's task and the engine's reader tasks both push.
- **Radio faces** (`Radio`, `WifiPhy`, `BlePhy`, and the face a `WifiPhy::into_face` mounts): all take channel frames without waiting (`Transport::forward_side`), through ndn-radio's **`SideLane`**, one per phy: a bounded queue of **64 frames and 128 KiB** (`Radio::with_side_limits`, `WifiPhy::with_side_limits`, `BlePhy::with_side_limits`) drained by **one writer task** that sends each frame as the phy's own NDN send does. A frame that finds no room is refused **`Full` at once** (the forwarder counts `sink_full`; the receiver sees a congested link's gap); a frame over the octet bound or the smallest picked MTU is `TooLarge`, one without the marker `Malformed`. A `Radio` over several phys queues a frame on **every picked phy or on none** (`Full` if any picked queue has no room; counted in `Radio::side_stats`). **The datagram class only:** a radio has no stream, so the stream class is `NotCarried` (counted `class_unsupported`), and so is a phy that cannot carry a side frame (a BLE phy with NDNts framing, `side_frame_max` 0, refuses both classes). **A channel flood does not starve NDN.** The writer and the NDN send path (`send_bytes`, the async `send_side`) take one send at a time through a **one-permit first-come first-served gate**, so a channel flood delays an NDN packet by at most the one frame in flight and an NDN flood delays a queued channel frame by one send; the lane fills and the forwarder sees `Full`. The control bypass (`send_robust`) stays outside the gate. A `WifiPhy` nobody has read from has no medium yet and refuses `Io` saying so.
- **The ring carrier** (`RingFace` in ndn-face-shm): a lossy broadcast ring of 256 slots of `frame_max + 256` octets (`frame_max` from 36 octets to 1 MiB) with one writer and at most 32 readers **at a time**. The writer is a send-only sink: `forward_side` publishes without waiting and is never full, a reader that falls more than 256 frames behind is lapped and counts it (`RingStats::lapped`), and only the datagram class is carried (a stream-class frame is `NotCarried`, counted `class_unsupported`). A reader maps the ring read-only and carries nothing out (`side_frame_max` is 0), so a local reader **cannot forge a frame**: what it reads is what the writer's process wrote. **On Linux the kernel enforces that against a reader of the same uid too:** every region of the ring is a `memfd` sealed against shrinking and growing, and the data region against any write (`F_SEAL_FUTURE_WRITE`), so no writable mapping, `write(2)` or `ftruncate` of it succeeds through any descriptor of it, a `/proc/self/fd/N` reopen with `O_RDWR` included (`EPERM`). Each reader is handed a registry page **of its own**, which the writer maps read-only: a reader writes only its own wake flag and cursor, cannot reach another reader's flag or the header a later attach reads, and cannot make the writer's mapping fault. A reader refuses a region its owner did not seal, and says so. Where there is no sealing (not Linux) the guarantee is across uids only (§13.12 item 19). A frame that is not marked or is under 36 octets is dropped and counted; a one-octet `0x00` frame is the writer's end. A reader finds the ring by a control socket named from the ring's name, and the handoff of its descriptors is gated by a capability token bound to a 32-octet secret (a mutual challenge, so the secret never crosses the socket). **The handshake is bounded:** each connection's runs in a task of its own and ends after 5 s in all (`HANDSHAKE_TIMEOUT`, on both ends), at most 16 at a time and 8 for one uid, so a peer that connects and sends nothing, or one octet at a time, holds one place for 5 s and never the accept loop; a client whose owner is silent gets `NotServing` ("did not answer within 5 s") and does not wait. **The handoff never stops serving because of failed attempts:** a connection that fails at any step (a reader that authenticates and leaves, a socket error, a refusal) is logged with why and what to do, and the loop goes on. A reader's place is freed when it leaves (nothing holds the read end of its wake pipe), so a ring serves any number of readers over its life; when all 32 places are held by live readers an attach is refused at once, `Declined`: "ring has no free reader slots … ask the owner to restart it". The handoff counts failed attempts per peer credential (uid and pid from `SO_PEERCRED`; peers whose credential cannot be read share one bucket) and, because a process can fork to take a new pid, also per uid in a larger bound. A wrong proof, or a handshake not finished in time, is an attempt; a connection that closes without a word (a writer probing whether the name is live) is not. A process with 16 failed attempts, or a uid with 64, inside a 60 s window (monotonic, from the first failure) is closed without a word read, authorized or not, until the window ends, with a `warn` once and a `debug` per refusal that say who and when; other peers are served throughout. The table of counters holds at most 1,024 entries and drops the oldest. This bounds availability only: who may attach is decided by the token alone, never by this table or a clock. A user who can run a process as the same uid as a reader can still keep that uid out for one window; that uid already holds whatever the token protects.
- **TLV-framed streams** (a TCP socket, and a stream face made without a side lane): `NotCarried`. The codec finds the end of a frame from its first octets read as a TLV header, and the marker is TLV-TYPE 0 followed by a byte read as a length, so a side frame written as is would put the reader out of step with the NDN packets behind it.
- **The IPC socket face** (`IpcFace`, the daemon's unix socket and named pipe, and the client end `ipc_face_connect`): both classes, `side_frame_max` 1 MiB once the client has opted in (below), as a **side record** beside the NDN packets of the stream. The record is `0x00` (the marker) | the ingress face id (8 octets, big-endian; `u64::MAX` when none: the app end sends none, since the daemon knows the face a frame came over and never reads it) | the length of the frame (4 octets, big-endian) | the frame, which starts with its own `0x00`. The stream's decoder tells a record from an NDN packet by the first octet (a TLV element never starts with `0x00`: TLV-TYPE 0 is not assigned and a longer type starts `0xFD`, `0xFE` or `0xFF`), so ordinary NDN traffic on the socket is byte for byte what it was. **It fails closed:** a record with a length over 1 MiB, one whose frame does not start with the marker (seen as soon as that octet arrives), a record that arrives before the peer's hello, and a stream that ends inside a record are errors that close the face with the reason; nothing is skipped or guessed at. Frames go through one bounded queue per face (1,024 frames and 4 MiB) written whole under the face's writer lock by one pump task the face's owner runs (`ndn-face` spawns it where it makes the face), so a record is never split by an NDN packet: `send_side` waits for room, `forward_side_from` (the engine's path to a sink) never waits and is refused `Full` when there is none (counted `sink_full`), and `Closed` once the face is gone. The daemon passes the ingress (`forward_side_from(frame, class, ingress)`); the client end hands it to `recv_side`. A client end holds the frames the daemon routes to it in a side inbox of 256 frames and 8 MiB like an in-process app's, and drops (counting `ForwarderClient::side_dropped`) what finds it full. **Opt-in: the hello.** A client built before the record reads its socket as TLV elements, and one record would put it out of step for good. So **a daemon sends a record only to a client that said it reads them, and refuses every frame to any other** (`NotCarried`, `side_frame_max` 0 on that face, so the forwarder installs no route to it and sends it the session Data as a plain Data without the label; counted `refused_not_carried`). The client says it with the **hello**, a record whose length is 0 (13 octets: marker, `u64::MAX`, 0; it carries no frame). `Connection::open_side` (`ForwarderClient::open_side`) writes it, and `attach_forwarder` calls it, so a node's connection has said it before the node asks for or answers anything: the hello is written before any other octet the client writes after the call, whichever of the pump and an NDN send writes first (both take it under the writer lock), so it precedes the Interest that makes the client a sink. The daemon's end opens its lane when it reads the hello; a client end opens when it writes it; until then both refuse and say 0. A record that arrives on a face that has not heard a hello is an error that closes the face. A client that only wants NDN traffic sends no hello and never meets a record. **Skew:** a client that opted in against a daemon built before the record is closed by the daemon on the hello (type 0 and a length of `u64::MAX` read as TLV exceeds the frame limit), which the client sees as a closed connection; update the daemon.
