- With the sender's and the receiver's threads pinned to one core and the main thread to another the run is stable: 35.0 µs against 30.2 µs, a ratio of 1.16.
- A per-ping timer makes no difference: 39.9 µs against 40.3 µs.
- An earlier attribution of 4.5 µs "outside `Heap::emit`" does not exist: measured, it is 0.03 µs before and 0.06 µs after the receiver change, so it was cold-start cost of the measurement.

**Throughput.** From 128 octets up the writer thread carries two AEAD passes over every octet: the channel's seal of the frame (§11.2) and quinn's own encryption of the packet, on one worker that also runs quinn's driver. The raw row does only the second. Removing the channel's seal entirely in a scratch run (not shipped) gives the ceiling: the sender's frames a second rise from 82.5 k to 103 k at 1 KiB (+25 %) and from 91.5 k to 107–121 k at 8 KiB, which is about 0.61 times the raw datagram at 1 KiB and about 0.72–0.82 times the raw stream at 8 KiB. So the 0.9 targets at 1 KiB and 8 KiB cannot be met with two AEAD passes on one worker. At 64 octets the same scratch run reaches 1.95–1.98 M messages/s against 1.79–1.87 M with the seal in, so the 1.94 M compared against is reachable only barely even with a free seal.

### 16.4 What the tuning changed in the specified behaviour

1. **The stream frame limit (§13.8, §11.6).** At `path_mtu_max` 1,452, 8 KiB messages over 256 paths stalled: 0 messages in the window. The cause was a stream frame of 1,049,252 octets, built from 128 queued messages, over the 1,048,576 octets the face grants. The face refused it, the refusal was taken as a dead stream, and every later emission rebuilt and re-sealed a 1 MiB frame (AES-GCM was 60 % of the sender thread). `Carrier::stream_frame_max()` is the fix: the stream lane packs to the smaller of it and the channel's own limit, and a message whose frame fits no frame the stream takes is refused `TooLarge` at `emit`. After it the same row is 54.7 k messages/s. The limits are now one constant: `ndn_transport::side_header::STREAM_FRAME_GRANT` (1 MiB) is the face's stream-frame limit and the channel's stream-frame default, and the message default is derived from it (the grant less 1 KiB), because a message at the earlier default was refused at `emit` (`TooLarge`, size 1,048,624, max 1,048,576), which the test `a_message_of_the_default_limit_fits_a_frame_the_face_grants` caught.
2. **The ring hashes lazily (§13.2 item 9).** The checkpoint hash was a sixth of the writer thread at 1 KiB and 8 KiB (16–17 % of its samples). Recording a frame now keeps a reference and the hash is computed when the ring is drained. Interleaved before and after, 6 pairs: 64 B ×1.03, 1 KiB ×1.16 (506 k to 571 k), 8 KiB ×1.18 (74 k to 86 k).
3. **The receiver keeps its last channel.** A run of frames for one channel takes no session lookup. The saving is small and was not separable in latency: no detectable difference over 24 interleaved pairs.
4. **The idle write is one lock round.** An emitter that writes inline decides in the same lock round that commits its frame whether the lane is idle; the earlier path packed again and took a third lock. The per-frame message counter is one add. An isolated message asks for no call back (§13.8); that change had no measurable end-to-end effect (p50 median −0.5 µs over 20 interleaved pairs).

### 16.5 Tried and removed

**Authenticate-only for unsealed spaces.** The idea was to send the frames of an unsealed (public) space under a MAC and no encryption. It was measured and **dropped**: every frame in every space stays AEAD sealed (AES-256-GCM; ChaCha20-Poly1305 only where `ring` does not build or the CPU has no AES), and nothing of the work reached the wire, the codec or the sources. The reason is cost on the measuring host (AMD EPYC 7532: AES-NI and PCLMULQDQ, no VAES; `ring` 0.17): **no MAC beats `ring`'s AES-GCM seal above about 150 octets**, because the seal is one stitched AES-NI and PCLMUL kernel and every MAC has to process the whole frame as additional data, which `ring` does not stitch. ns a frame, medians:

| octets | AES-256-GCM seal | AES-GMAC (`ring`) | ChaCha20-Poly1305 tag only (`ring`) | keyed BLAKE3 to 16 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 64 | 157 | 113 | 188 | 133 |
| 1,200 | 484 | 1,033 | 621 | 1,498 |
| 8,962 | 2,647 | 7,364 | 3,615 | 3,340 |
| 65,000 | 18,410 | 52,645 | 25,169 | 20,643 |

At 8,962 octets, the size the gates run at, GMAC is 2.8 times the seal, a ChaCha tag-only 1.37 times and BLAKE3 1.26 times. Only at 64 octets does a MAC win (GMAC 113 against 157 ns). Only on a CPU without AES (and on wasm32), where the seal is ChaCha, would a ChaCha tag-only be cheaper than the seal: 8–18 % (0.82–0.92 of ChaCha's own seal, from 64 octets to 1 MiB). That is not the host the gates run on, and nothing was built for it.

**Taking the seal off the writer thread.** Two designs were built, measured against the shipped one in interleaved pairs (medians, host load 4–8), and **removed** because each was slower. Messages/s, shipped then variant:

| design | 64 B | 1 KiB | 8 KiB |
|---|---|---|---|
| the whole drain on a writer thread | 1,825 k to 739 k | 558 k to 461 k | 86 k to 57 k |
| seal one frame ahead on a seal thread | 1,792 k to 1,680 k | 619 k to 430 k | 93 k to 61 k |

The cause is that a drain is about one frame long on a single worker, so nothing overlaps: the seal is not hidden and every frame pays a hand-over between threads. The first design also made the QUIC driver wake across threads (48 % of the sender thread was in libc); the second spent about a quarter of its time waiting on the seal thread. The latency with and without an offload was not measured, because none shipped.

**The copies.** A read of the path from `Heap::emit` to quinn found one copy of a message's payload by the channel (into the frame buffer, which batching needs) and none for the seal, which is in place, nor for the datagram carrier. The one copy that could go is in ndn-ext: a stream frame of 16 KiB or less is copied once behind its 4-octet length prefix. It was not changed.

### 16.6 Where this leaves the gates

These verdicts are **superseded by the performance pass (§18)**: every gate was measured again at the pins and again after the pass (§18.2), the causes of the gaps were attributed by profile (§18.3, §18.4) and what remains is stated with numbers (§18.5). The figures of §16.2 to §16.5 stay as the record of the tuning pass.

**The owner's decision** was to finish stages 4–7 first and then to do **one focused performance pass**; §18 is that pass.

### 16.7 Stages 4 and 5

**Method.** The **baseline** is the stage-3 tree of §16.1, ndn-rs `33a2fd95` with ndn-ext `407bbaf`, built in scratch worktrees; **current** is the tree with stages 4 and 5. Pinned to cores 10 to 13 (`taskset -c 10-13`), 3 s a row, baseline and current alternating in pairs with the order flipped each pair. The whole table was run twice, before and after the fix of `ASK_NEW` (§3.1), and the two runs agree. The host was shared and **busier than §16.1's**: the 1-minute load was 11.7 to 16.5 (median 14.5). **Read every figure as ±5 %.** The raw logs are not in the repository.

**One link, `chan_ping`, baseline against current** (the rung of §16.1; the nodes use the one-link carrier of that rung, not a forwarder):

| row | baseline | current | ratio of medians | pair ratios |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 8 B messages/s | 2.151 M | 1.882 M | 0.875 | .92 .90 .86 |
| 64 B messages/s | 1.909 M | 1.681 M | 0.881 | .84 .90 .87 |
| 1 KiB messages/s | 626 k | 539 k | 0.861 | .87 .84 .87 |
| 8 KiB messages/s | 91.0 k | 82.7 k | 0.909 | .92 .84 .93 |
| 64 B half round trip p50, cold (14 pairs) | 33.3 µs | 33.0 µs | 0.985 | range 0.61 to 1.60 |
| 64 B half round trip p50, hot (14 pairs) | 34.1 µs | 41.0 µs | 1.21 | range 0.96 to 1.98 |
| 64 B p99, cold / hot | 52.0 / 50.8 µs | 48.8 / 63.6 µs | | |
| per-message CPU, sender (`poll_next` / `next_batch`) | 532 / 448 ns | 553 / 467 ns | 1.040 / 1.043 | |
| per-message CPU, receiver (`poll_next` / `next_batch`) | 304 / 276 ns | 314 / 279 ns | 1.033 / 1.009 | |

- **Throughput is 0.86 to 0.91 of the baseline at every size.** It is real (both runs, every pair). The first run read 0.83 to 0.90.
- **Hot latency read +18 % and +21 % in the two runs. Cold latency is not separable:** the first run read +21 %, the second −1.5 %, and the range of the pair ratios is wide.
- **Per-message CPU moves 1 to 4 %.** That row uses a carrier with no stream, so it carries no checkpoints.
- **The raw carrier is unchanged**, which clears the carrier and the host: a separate `carrier_bench` control (defaults, MTU 1,452, so not comparable to §16.2's raw rows) read 28.5 µs cold and 28.9 µs hot, and baseline against current throughput 0.974 (1 KiB datagram), 0.999 (1 KiB stream) and 1.009 (8 KiB stream). The channel against the raw half round trip: cold 1.16 in both trees, hot 1.17 in the baseline and 1.39 now.
- **The cause of the receiver's share was found by the performance pass (§18.3):** `Verify::forget_farthest` and `Verify::judge` walked every settled slot of the 4,096-slot store on every frame and every checkpoint (14 % of all samples at 64 B). The 0.86 to 0.91 itself was not measured again against a stage-3 build.
- **An oddity, not diagnosed:** in the current tree the per-message row delivers 99.97 to 99.99 % of the messages (the baseline 100 %), and the shortfall grows with the run [INFERENCE: a non-data frame takes a tap's release slot and `Latest` supersedes that round].

**The cost of checkpoints** was measured in a forwarder-attached direct shape (a sender and a receiver, each on its own forwarder, over one real QUIC link), comparing the default period of 20 ms with the largest, 2,000 ms, 3 interleaved pairs. It is the **difference** between two periods, not against a build with no checkpoints, and it includes the stream writes, the wake-ups and the hashing of frames, not only the signatures. Share of one core:

| rate (64 B) | sender, 20 ms | sender, 2,000 ms | receiver, 20 ms | receiver, 2,000 ms | difference |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1,000 messages/s | 3.75 % | 3.87 % | 5.54 % | 3.83 % | sender −0.12, **receiver +1.71** |
| 100 messages/s | 1.48 % | 1.11 % | 1.77 % | 1.04 % | sender +0.37, **receiver +0.73** |

- **The design target** was under 0.5 % of a core for each active sender, on both sides. **The sender meets it** (+0.37 % at worst). **The receiver missed it:** +0.73 % at 100 messages/s and +1.71 % at 1,000. After the performance pass the receiver is at +0.52 % and +0.69 % (§18.6).
- **Throughput** in the same shape at 20 ms is 0.75 (64 B) and 0.77 (1 KiB) of the 2,000 ms run; the 64 B p50 is 39.6 against 35.9 µs and p99 56 against 46 µs. Part of the 2 s gain is probably that the sender's ring evicts frames before they are hashed, so it is not a clean cost of signing [INFERENCE: the cost follows the rate of frames, which is frame hashing, §15.3].

**Through a relay** (`chan_relay`: a sender, a relay and receivers A and B, each on its own forwarder, joined by real QUIC links; the relay holds no identity, trust or data layer, only routes and the side table; forwarder-attached nodes). Medians of 3 runs (14 for the latency rows); ranges in brackets:

| | direct | one relay, one receiver | one relay, two receivers (A / B) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 64 B messages/s | 1.276 M | 529 k [527 k, 715 k] | 949 k / 981 k, 1.91 M in all |
| 1 KiB messages/s | 208 k | 146 k [95 k, 189 k] | 108 k / 120 k |
| 64 B half round trip p50 | 36.2 µs | 72.0 µs | 83.9 µs |
| 1 KiB half round trip p50 | 45.5 µs | 97.9 µs | 86.4 µs |

- **One relay hop roughly doubles the half round trip, and that is one more QUIC hop, not the table.** Pass of the performance work, interleaved pairs on four pinned cores at host load 30-50: the relay adds about 28 µs (relay1 minus direct, 4 pairs: 60, 18, 28, 27 µs), which is what one raw QUIC datagram hop costs on the same cores at the same time (`carrier_bench` lat: 28-39 µs). The relay runtime does, for each frame, a wake from `epoll`, `recvmmsg` (and one more that finds nothing), the quinn receive, the reader task, `on_frame`, `forward`, the quinn send and its `sendmsg`, and the standalone acknowledgement quinn sends for each packet (4.4 more `epoll_wait`, 5.3 more `recvmmsg` and 2.7 more `sendmsg` per round trip than the direct shape: `strace -c -f`). Of the relay thread's user samples 6 % are code of this repository (side table, face reader, link face, 0.15-0.8 % for `SideTable::on_frame`): under 1 µs of the 28.
- **The sender's link carries one copy of each frame** with two receivers behind the relay (the relay's frames out are twice its frames in; a test asserts it). Each receiver gets about what the single one does, so the total is about twice.
- **The relay rows are noisy**, and one relay against two inverts on throughput and on the 1 KiB latency: read no order into them. At saturation the receivers' app-face inboxes also drop (`sink_full`). **Why relay1 64 B throughput is under direct:** frames carry the same 52 messages in both shapes, but behind the relay they arrive in bursts, and a `Latest` receiver keeps one value per path, so a burst over 256 paths is conflated before the consumer polls (at 4,096 paths relay1 delivers 905 k against direct 960-1,050 k; at 256 paths 490 k against 1.76 M). At 1 KiB the relay thread is the first to saturate (it decrypts and encrypts every 9 kB frame, AES-GCM is 44 % of its user samples): the kernel drops what the sender's link sent and it never read (relay table frames in / sender link frames out 0.46-0.55); with two worker threads on the relay runtime (`chan_relay relay_workers=2`) it is 0.83 and 64 B relay1 rises from 438 k to 580 k.
- **The face reader yields after a frame a sink refused** (a link whose send buffer the reader filled without letting the link's task run): relay frames out / in 0.88 → 0.99 at 64 B, 64 B at 4,096 paths +15 %; the fan-out still never blocks.
- **The 1 KiB gap to the one-link row (208 k against 539 k)** was the default datagram frame of a forwarder-attached node, 1,200 octets (`DATAGRAM_FRAME_MAX`): a 1 KiB message filled a frame, where the one-link carrier packs several. The limit is now read from the sinks' faces (§14.10): direct shape 1 KiB 150 k → 273 k messages/s (6 interleaved pairs, medians; the one-link row in the same minutes 231 k), relay1 85 k → 142 k, send CPU per delivered message 8.5 → 5.3 µs.

**Rung C3: one sender, four receivers behind the relay** (SHAPES-CHANNEL.md §13, stage 4 gate). This rung is measured in ndn-ext, not in versus (`ndn-ext/crates/faces/ndn-face-quic/examples/chan_relay.rs`, shape `relay4`; the fact is also asserted by `tests/chan_relay.rs::four_receivers_behind_a_relay_get_every_message_and_the_sender_link_carries_one_copy_of_each_frame`). Method: the topology of the relay rows with receivers A to D (each on a forwarder and a runtime of its own, a QUIC link each to the relay, one link from the relay to the sender), run as `chan_relay shapes=direct,relay1,relay4 only=c3:64:400,thr:64,lat:64 repeat=3 secs=3`: the `c3` row sends 400 messages of 64 B in windows of 20, each window delivered to all four receivers before the next, and compares the frame counters of the sender's link, the relay's table and its links; `thr` is the back-to-back rate each receiver's `Items` yields; `lat` is the half round trip of each receiver in turn (the sender pings, that receiver pongs, the other three receive and count the pings), 10,000 timed round trips after 1,000 warm-up, as in the rows above (hot only: the example has no cold row). **Measured on a shared host, not pinned, load average 15 to 17 (the example was run without `taskset`); the repetitions differ by far more than ±5 %, so read the ranges, not the medians.**

- **The sender link carries one copy of each frame: PASS in all three repetitions.** For 400 messages the sender's link sent 802 frames (the messages and the checkpoints and `HELLO` that followed), the relay received 802 and sent 3,208 = 4 × 802, each of the four relay links sent 802 and each receiver's link received 802; every receiver got all 400 messages. The same row with one receiver behind the relay (`relay1`) and with the direct link gives 803 / 802 frames out of the sender, so the sender's cost does not grow with the receivers.
- **Throughput, 64 B**, medians of 3 [min, max]: A 410 k [394 k, 816 k], B 583 k [389 k, 833 k], C 440 k [437 k, 446 k], D 567 k [387 k, 739 k] messages/s, 1.98 M [1.63 M, 2.83 M] in all; one receiver behind a relay 909 k [905 k, 921 k]; direct 1.20 M [875 k, 1.27 M]. The sender emitted 1.1 to 1.5 M messages/s in each; the receivers' app-face inboxes drop at saturation, so the per-receiver figures are shares of that.
- **Half round trip, 64 B p50 [min, max] (p99 median)**: direct 53.2 µs [51.4, 57.6] (106 µs); one relay, one receiver 102.5 µs [88.8, 134.0] (287 µs); four receivers A 111.8 µs [102.3, 160.0] (305 µs), B 133.6 µs [122.6, 170.7] (471 µs), C 161.4 µs [158.3, 166.9] (529 µs), D 97.7 µs [94.3, 148.0] (214 µs). The relay hop costs about 50 µs on the median over the direct link on this host. The four-receiver medians sit between 98 and 161 µs, about one relay hop above the direct link for the best and about two for the worst; the ranges of the repetitions overlap those of the one-receiver row, so **the rung does not show the fourth receiver's latency to be worse than one relay hop gives for the first**, and it does not show it to be equal either: a quiet, pinned run is needed to decide that, and none was made. The direct figure here (53 µs) is above the one-link row of the table above (36.2 µs) [INFERENCE: because of the load of this run], so no gate against stage 3's latency is claimed from these numbers.

**Not run.** N1 (there is no such harness in ndn-ext: the `ndn-link` ladder measures something else) and any radio path (the radio sinks, §14.9, are tested in ndn-radio against test bearers; no channel was measured over a radio). `Trust::Verified`'s latency was measured by the performance pass (§18.6); a real network interface was not measured.

**The gates.** The gate of stage 4 and 5 that the one-link rung stays within 3 % of stage 3 (SHAPES-CHANNEL.md §13) was **not met** when this was measured: throughput 9 to 14 % down and hot latency up about 20 %; the checkpoint gate was met by the sender and missed by the receiver. **Superseded by §18** (the cause on the receiver, the cost of checkpoints after it, the relay).

### 16.8 What the figures do not cover

- A real network interface: every run is loopback.
- `Reliable` and `Window` (only `Latest` was measured, also in §18); and the setup's Interests crossing the link in the one-link rung (its two nodes share one forwarder; the relay rows of §16.7 do cross the links). `Trust::Verified` is measured in §18.6.
- Zenoh was rerun on this host at the load of the pass in §18 (same pairs as the channel); §16.2 to §16.7 compare with the design note's figures (§1.2), taken at another load. Zenoh was not measured at 128 B to 512 B.
- The table after the changes since the tuning pass is §18.2.
- A quiet host: the figures of §16 were taken at load 5–6 on a shared host, and single latency pairs vary by ±5 µs; §18 was taken at load 12 to 57 (§18.1).

## 17. The verbs: put, emit, get

A space has three verbs and a point read. This section states what `get` does with the first two, exactly; [heap.md](heap.md) §10.3 and §10.4 state the kept side (the register, `watch`).

| Verb | What it is | Who may call it |
|---|---|---|
| `put(path, bytes, opts)` | **kept**: signed by the writer's device key, chained, stored, synced; the register's head of `path` ([heap.md](heap.md) §10.1) | a writer (§4.2, [heap.md](heap.md) §10.3) |
| `emit(path, bytes, opts)` | **ephemeral**: sent over the space's channel (§13.1), never an entry, stored and served by nobody | a writer (§13.1) |
| `get(what, opts)` | **one stream**: the kept values of the space, then their changes, merged with the emissions of its channels, filtered by `what` | a reader or a writer; a follower for kept values only |
| `kept(path)` | the head's value now, from what this node holds; never waits, never touches the network | a reader or a writer |

```rust
pub fn get(&self, what: Select, opts: GetOptions) -> Result<Items, HeapError>;
pub async fn kept(&self, path: &Name) -> Result<Option<Item>, HeapError>;

pub enum Select { Path(Name), Prefix(Name) }
pub struct GetOptions {          // Clone, Debug, PartialEq, Eq: not Copy (it holds an Ask)
    pub kept: bool,              // kept values and their changes (default true)
    pub heads: Heads,            // Heads::Winner (default) | Heads::All (§17.9); kept values only
    pub live: Live,              // Live::Emitted (default): also emissions; Live::Off
    pub modes: Modes,            // which channel modes to join (default: all the senders offer)
    pub trust: Trust,            // Trust::Prompt (default) | Trust::Verified (§15.8); emissions only
    pub congestion: Congestion,  // Block (default) | Drop; Reliable and Window emissions only
    pub ask: Option<Ask>,        // None (default): no query; Some: ask the space's writers (§17.10, query.md)
}
pub enum Heads { Winner, All }
pub struct Items;                // Stream<Item = Result<Got, GetNotice>>
impl Items {
    pub async fn next(&mut self) -> Option<Result<Got, GetNotice>>;
    pub fn next_batch(&mut self, buf: &mut Vec<Got>) -> usize;
    pub async fn caught_up(&mut self);
    pub fn hello_seen(&self) -> bool;
}
pub struct Got { pub path: Name, pub bytes: Bytes, pub by: Name, pub writer: Name, pub kind: Kind }
#[non_exhaustive]
pub enum Kind { Kept(Kept), Emitted(Emitted), Answered(Answered) }   // Answered: a query's answer (§17.10)
#[non_exhaustive]
pub struct Kept { pub id: EntryId, pub as_of: Option<Duration>, pub removed: bool,
                  pub winner: bool, pub supersedes: Vec<EntryId> }
pub struct Emitted { pub seq: u64, pub stands: Option<Duration> }
pub struct Answered { pub query: QueryId, pub via: Via, pub refusal: Option<AppRefusal>, pub proof: Option<Bytes> }
#[non_exhaustive]
pub enum GetNotice {
    Lagged { missed: u64 },                          // emissions the mode dropped, or answers dropped from a full queue (§17.5, §17.10)
    Withdrawn { id: EntryId, why: Refusal },         // a kept entry this stream gave was refused after the fact
    LiveEnded(LiveEnd),                              // the emission part ended (§12.7); kept values go on
    KeptEnded { why: String },                       // the kept part ended (§17.7)
    Retracted { writer: Name, from: u64, to: u64 },  // a checkpoint contradicted emissions (§15.5)
    HeadsEnded { path: Name, ids: Vec<EntryId> },    // §17.9
    HeadsCapped { path: Name, hidden: u32 },         // §17.9
    QueryFailed { server: Name, why: QueryError },   // one server of a query failed (§17.10)
    AnswersDone { asked: u32, answered: u32, refused: u32, failed: u32, silent: u32, not_asked: u32 },   // told once (§17.10)
}
```

### 17.1 What `get` creates

`get` is synchronous and does no network I/O. When it returns, **both feeds are registered**: the kept watcher (the register's heads under `what` are listed and the watcher registered in one step under the space's lock, so no change falls between them) and, with `Live::Emitted`, the emission sink (§12.7). With `GetOptions::ask` it also registers the **answer queue** and **spawns** the query on the node's tasks (§17.10), still with no I/O in the caller's task. Nothing that happens after the call is lost for want of a first poll. `get` asks no sender for a session: that is `ask_channel_in`, or the announce-driven ask of a device that is listening (§9). Dropping the `Items` closes the feeds, releases the sink's sessions (§14.10) and **cancels the query**.

### 17.2 `kind` is the authority

Every item says what it is. **`Kind::Kept`** is durable and signed: the head of its path in the register, an entry the writer's device signed, chained, and that this node judged ([heap.md](heap.md) §3.8, §9). **`Kind::Emitted`** is neither: a frame that opened under the channel's key is **a key holder's** (every reader of a sealed space holds the key), and it is **the sender's** only once a signed checkpoint of the sender lists it (§15); `Emitted::verified` says which of the two was known **when the item was given** (§17.6). It is not an entry, and **it is never the current value of a path**: no emission replaces, hides or confirms a kept value. An application that needs "the value" reads `Kept`; one that wants "what is being done now" (a cursor, a drag) reads `Emitted`. A `stand` replay (§13.6) is an `Emitted` with `stands` set to what is left of the stated lifetime; it is not kept either.

### 17.3 The order

1. **The kept listing comes first, always**: every path under `what` that has a value, as the register's head, in path order (canonical NDN order of the path), each as `Kind::Kept` with `removed: false`. A path with no value (never written, or removed) has no item. A value in pieces that is not whole here is not listed; it is given as a kept change when its last piece is held. **While an entry of a sealed space is held closed** (its epoch key is not held yet, so the path it writes or removes is unknown) there is no listing: the register may show an older head of that path as current, as `kept` refuses for the same reason, so `get` is refused `NotReady` with the same reason (`kept` and `get` give the same text: the key is being fetched; try again).
2. **`caught_up()` resolves after the listing was yielded**: when every path that had a value when `get` returned has been given by `next` or `next_batch`. Called before the listing was read, it reads the rest of it into the `Items`' own buffer (the next `next` gives it, before anything else): it never waits for the caller. At once when `kept` is `false`.
3. **Emissions and answers that arrive during the listing are held** (emissions queued in the sink under the bounds of §17.5, answers in the answer queue of §17.10) **and come after it**: nothing emitted or answered is yielded before the last item of the listing.
4. **After the listing, the sources alternate fairly** (kept, emitted and answered; the two of them that exist in a stream without a query): the poll starts after the source taken last, so the one not taken last is taken first among those with something queued, and none starves another. **No order is promised between a kept item and an emitted or an answered one**: an emission or an answer made after a `put` can reach a receiver before the `put` does (kept data travels by sync, emissions by the channel, answers by the signed path of [query.md](query.md)), and the reverse.
5. **Within a source**: kept items are in the order the register applied the changes of this node, and **never an older value of a path after a newer one**; emitted items are in the mode's order (§12.6): `Reliable` and `Window` in sequence order, `Latest` the newest per sender and name, never an older after a newer; across channels, arrival order.
6. **A notice comes after the items queued before it in its own source**: `Lagged`, `LiveEnded` and `Retracted` after the emissions (§12.7), `Withdrawn` after the kept items, `QueryFailed`, `AnswersDone` and the `Lagged` of dropped answers after the answers (§17.10).

A joiner therefore gets the **current kept state** (the listing), then what changes, and with it what is being emitted, without a connect step and without a gap between the listing and the changes.

### 17.4 Duplicates

- **A path both put and emitted** gives **two items**: one `Kept` and one `Emitted`. They are never merged and never deduplicated, even when their bytes are equal. An emission is never suppressed by a `put` (there is no mark between the two): a stale emission after a later `put` of the same writer is delivered, and `kind` tells the application which one is the value.
- **A kept entry is yielded once per `Items`.** The `Items` remembers the entry it gave at each path, and never gives the same entry again, also not after a lag (§17.5). An application that is told an entry twice (the same `Kept::id`) by an implementation that repeats the current value after a lag may treat it as the same value; it is never told an **older** value of a path after a newer one.
- A `stand` replay is `Emitted` with `stands`, as §17.2; it never becomes `Kept`.
- **A removal** is `Kind::Kept` with `removed: true`, empty `bytes` and `id` the entry that removed the path (a removal entry that is the head of the path); when no entry removes it (its last head was **refused after it was given** and nothing it superseded is left, so `Withdrawn` is told for it too), `id` is the entry this `Items` gave last at that path, the one refused: the register has no removing entry to name. It is given only for a path this `Items` gave a value of; a path that never had a value in this stream gives none.
- **`Withdrawn { id, why }`** is given for an entry this `Items` gave and that was the value it had given at its path when it was refused ([heap.md](heap.md) §3.8, §10.4): drop what was derived from `id`. The entry the refused one superseded returns as the next `Kept` (or, with none left, a removal).

### 17.5 A slow reader

**Kept: never lost.** The kept queue holds at most `Config::watch_queue` changes (4,096 by default), as a `watch`'s does, and **only what the feed can tell**: the changes of values under `what` and the withdrawals of entries (which name no path). Changes of other paths, members changes and alerts are **not queued**, so a writer's flood of them, or an alert flood, cannot make a feed over a narrow selector overflow. When a wanted change does not fit, the stream is **not told a loss**: when it has read what was queued, it takes the space's lock, forgets what was dropped, and in the same step reads from the store the heads under `what`. It then gives, in path order and before any later change, every head that is not the entry it already gave at its path, and a removal for every path it gave a value of that has none now. So **every path's current value is delivered**, and a path that changed several times while the reader was behind is given its **latest** value and never an older one after a newer. The intermediate values are skipped **by state**, not lost data: the log and `watch` ([heap.md](heap.md) §10.4) have the history. A slow reader never slows a `put`, a fetch or another reader. The read of the heads costs the lock and a scan of every head under `what` (§13.12 item 30). A reader that was removed meanwhile, or a space with an entry held closed, ends the feed instead (§17.7).

**Emitted: as the mode says** (§12.6): `Latest` keeps the newest per sender and name (coalescing is not a loss and is not told) in at most 4,096 pairs and 1 MiB charged, evicts the oldest pair past the bound and tells `Lagged { missed }`; `Reliable` and `Window` queue at most 1 MiB, and `Congestion::Block` (the default) **stalls the stream** so that the back pressure reaches the sender and nothing is lost, `Congestion::Drop` discards the oldest and tells `Lagged { missed }` in its place. **Kept items are unaffected**: the two sources have separate queues; a stalled or overflowing emission queue delays no kept item, and the other way round.

### 17.6 `by` and `writer`

`writer` is the device that signed the entry (kept) or sent the channel (emitted). For `Kept`, `by` is the **authenticated** person of that device: the namespace of the trust context that validated the signing key of the entry ([heap.md](heap.md) §9.1), which the node derived when it admitted the entry. For `Emitted`, `by` is the person of the sender's device **as this node judged it when the session was set up** (§3.4), kept with the session: it is the person the *channel* belongs to, **not proof that this frame came from that device**. A frame is the sender's only when a signed checkpoint of the sender lists it (§15). `Emitted::verified` is `true` when that was so **before the item was given** (`Trust::Verified`: nothing else is given) and `false` for `Trust::Prompt`, which is given the frame as soon as it opens, before any checkpoint can list it. A `Prompt` item is **never** marked verified afterwards: the receiver does not revisit what it gave (a contradicting checkpoint ends in a `Retracted` notice, §15.5, and a frame no checkpoint ever covers, on a channel that sends none, stays `false`). So for a `Prompt` item `by` and `writer` are a key holder's claim for at most the window `W` (§15.9) and, on a channel with no checkpoints, for good (§13.12 items 1 and 31); an application that must attribute an emission to a writer reads with `Trust::Verified` or reads `Kept`. A `Got` of either kind for the same writer has the same `by` unless the trust contexts changed in between.

`Got` carries the two as one `author: Arc<Author>` (`author.by`, `author.writer`; they were `Got::by` and `Got::writer` before §18.10). For an emission the `Author` is built **once, when the receive session is taken** (`ChannelRx::new`), from the person the node judged for the sender's device at that moment, and every emission of the session clones that `Arc`: as before the change, `by` is **kept with the session** and a renewal of the same session (§3.6) does not re-judge it, so the sentence above, "as this node judged it when the session was set up", is exact. A session taken anew (the sender's session ended and the receiver asked again, or the channel was replaced) builds a new `Author`. A kept value or an answer builds its own `Author` from the person judged when it is given, and shares nothing with an earlier one (§18.10 item 2).

### 17.7 Refusals: who may call what

`get` refuses at creation, each with a reason and a next step ([heap.md](heap.md) §10.5):

- **`BadRequest`** for `kept: false` with `Live::Off` and no `ask`: it asks for nothing; ask for kept values, emissions, a query, or a combination. And for an `ask` that is over a limit or names what is refused, each with its reason: a query over 2,048 octets (1,024 on `QueryPath::Channel`), a deadline of zero or over 10 s, `answer_max` of zero or over 4,096 (65,536 on `Auto` and `Channel`), a path over 255 octets, `Targets::Devices` empty, naming more than 16 or a name that is no device, or this device, and `QueryPath::Channel` with a proof or a frame over 1,150 octets ([query.md](query.md) §3.2).
- **`NotAllowed`** for `Live::Emitted` on a space this device only follows: a follower takes no part in channels; open the space as a member, or read the kept values with `Live::Off`. A follower **may** read kept values with `Live::Off`.
- **`Sealed`** (`AskOwner`) for kept values of a sealed space whose audience does not list this device's person: a **non-member**, or a member that was **removed** (as soon as this node holds the record that removes her), gets no kept value. With `kept: false` the call succeeds (it does no I/O), and a non-member gets **no emission** either: no sender answers her ask (§3.4, §4.1), so she holds no session. **An `Items` that is open when its reader is removed** ends its kept part at its next step: the fold moved, so it checks the reader once (one atomic load when nothing moved) and, when she is none, gives nothing it had queued or read ahead, says `GetNotice::KeptEnded { why }` (the removal, and to ask the owner to add the person as a reader) and then ends; the emissions side goes on until its sessions end (§3.6). A feed whose queue overflowed ends the same way instead of re-reading the register, and also while an entry of a sealed space is held closed (`KeptEnded` says so: a new `get` is refused `NotReady` until the key arrives).
- **`NotAllowed`** (`AskOwner`) for an `ask` on a space this device only follows, or from a device whose key does not stand as a listed reader or writer; **`NotReady`** for an `ask` before the space's record is held ([query.md](query.md) §3.2).

`put` and `emit` need a writer (`NotAllowed` and `NotWriter` otherwise). `get` needs a reader or a writer, and so does a query (`ask`); `serve` needs a writer ([query.md](query.md) §3.1). `kept` answers as before ([heap.md](heap.md) §10.3).

### 17.8 What `get` does not do

It does not resume (`watch` with a `Frontier` does, [heap.md](heap.md) §10.4); it does not give history, only the current value of each path (of each head, with `Heads::All`, §17.9) and what changes after; it does not make a session (§17.1); it does not order an emission against a `put` (§17.3 item 4); and it adds nothing to the wire: no field of an entry or of a frame is new for it (a query, §17.10, is the separate signed packet of [query.md](query.md), or a `QUERY` frame of an `Ask` session, §11.5; no frame of §11.1 carries it).

### 17.9 Every head: `GetOptions::heads`

`GetOptions::heads` is `Heads::Winner` (the default, and everything above) or `Heads::All`. It changes **what the kept side tells and nothing else**: the emission side, the order of §17.3 items 2 to 4, the refusals of §17.7 and the wire are as they were. A follower may read every head with `Live::Off`, as it reads winners. This section states the rules of [heap.md](heap.md) §10.3 `get` items 6 to 8 as they apply to the order, the duplicates and the slow reader.

1. **The listing (§17.3 item 1)** is, per path under `what` in path order, **every head in rank order**, the winner first (rank is the signed-region digest, then device, then seq: [heap.md](heap.md) §10.1). Each head is its own `Kind::Kept`; `removed` is true for a removal that is a head (a concurrent removal and a put are a real fork), and `winner` is true for the head of highest rank and false for the others. A path with no head has no item. A value in pieces that is not whole here is not listed: it is told when its last piece is held (a head that does not win is gathered while such a stream is open). A sealed space with an entry held closed is refused as for `Winner`.
2. **After the listing, each change of a path's set of heads is told**: every head the stream does not hold as told is an item, in rank order, whether it wins or not. A head arriving that ranks below the winner is told (`Winner` mode is told nothing); a head that outranks the winner is told with `winner: true`. `winner` is a **snapshot at telling**: another head may outrank it later and the stream does not say so (the winner is a function of the head set; a reader that needs it now calls `kept`, and a merging reader needs none: it merges every head).
3. **`Kept::supersedes` (stream-relative).** The first item of a change lists the heads **this stream had told at the path** that are no longer among the heads told: the heads the new head superseded, by its `after` or by any chain of entries (a head `M2` that superseded `M` that superseded `A`, told to a reader that never saw `M` because its queue overflowed, lists `A`: the stream holds `A` and that is what it retires). A reader keeps `id → value`, deletes `supersedes`, inserts the item, and never holds a head the register has dropped. Later items of the same change list nothing. A head that was not told is never listed.
4. **`GetNotice::HeadsEnded { path, ids }`** is told when told heads leave the set and **no new item carries them**: a withdrawn entry with nothing to restore (told after `Withdrawn { id, why }` of §17.4, which a stream that told the entry also gets), or an entry that arrives already superseded and supersedes a told head. If a value in pieces that is not whole is what would replace them, they are retired with it when its last piece is held, not before. A superseded entry that **returns** (its superseder was withdrawn) is told again as an item, because the stream no longer holds it, and retires the withdrawn head in its `supersedes`.
5. **At most 64 heads per path (`HEADS_MAX`)** are told: the 64 of highest rank. A path with more says so with `GetNotice::HeadsCapped { path, hidden }`, told after the items of the listing or change when `hidden` changes (not at every change of the path, and not for a path whose heads fit): a gap is never silent, and it is not a `Lagged`, which is for emissions. A head that falls out of the 64 is retired in `supersedes` or `HeadsEnded` like any head that leaves the set, and comes back as an item if it returns inside it. A head beyond the 64 is told by no stream, so its pieces are not fetched for one (they are when it rises into the 64), and the values of the 64 are read ahead at most `Config::heads_read` octets at a time ([heap.md](heap.md) §10.3 `get` item 6). `Heap::kept_all` reads every head, also past 64, for the library that merges in rounds.
6. **Duplicates (§17.4).** A head is told once per `Items` for as long as it stays a head the stream holds; it is told again only after it left the set (`supersedes`, `HeadsEnded`) and returned. Two heads at one path are two items, never merged and never deduplicated, even when their bytes are equal (the library decides what equal heads mean: [heap.md](heap.md) §10.3 `put_merged`).
7. **A slow reader (§17.5)** loses no head. The queue holds one reference per changed path (the path, its 64 heads of highest rank and how many there are in all, never a value: a value is read from the store when it is told, so a writer's thousands of heads at one path cost a queue what 64 do), at most `Config::watch_queue`, and only for streams that asked for every head: **a `Winner` stream is never queued a head it does not tell**, so a flood of concurrent writes cannot make it read the heads again, and an `All` stream pays for the heads it asked for. When a wanted change does not fit, the stream reads every head under `what` in the same step that forgets what was dropped, and compares each path's heads with what it told, so it tells every head it did not tell, retires every one it holds that left, and does so in path order before any later change. The cost is the lock and a scan of every head under `what`, as for winners.

It adds nothing to the wire: no entry carries a field for heads (an entry's `after` already says what it supersedes), and the merge put, `put_merged`, signs an ordinary Put entry whose `after` lists exactly the heads its writer merged ([heap.md](heap.md) §10.3).

### 17.10 Asking: `GetOptions::ask`

`GetOptions::ask` is `None` by default: `get` is then exactly what §17.1 to §17.9 say. With `Some(Ask)` the same stream also carries a **query** to the devices of the space's writers and what they answer. The feature is specified in [query.md](query.md) (the API, the signed path and the channel path, sealing, the server's and the asker's judging, limits); this section states what it changes in the stream.

1. **A third source.** An answer is a `Kind::Answered(Answered { query, via, refusal, proof })` item: not durable, **not an entry, never the current value of a path** and never told again. `writer` is the answering server's device and `by` the person of the key that signed the answer. A handler's own refusal is an `Answered` with `refusal` set and empty `bytes`. `GetNotice::QueryFailed { server, why }` tells a server that failed (`why.next()` says what to do) and `GetNotice::AnswersDone { asked, answered, refused, failed, silent, not_asked }` is told **once**, when every server asked has resolved or the query's deadline has passed, with `asked = answered + refused + failed + silent` (query.md §10.3).
2. **The order** is §17.3's: the kept listing first, answers that arrive during it come after it, then the sources alternate. No order is promised between an answer and a kept or emitted item. `QueryFailed` and `AnswersDone` come after the answers queued before them and are never dropped.
3. **A query-only stream.** With `kept: false` and `live: Live::Off` and an `ask`, the stream carries the query alone and **ends** (`None`) once `AnswersDone` has been yielded. With kept values or emissions on, the stream goes on and `AnswersDone` is a notice. `caught_up()` is about the kept listing and is unaffected (at once when `kept` is `false`).
4. **A slow reader** (§17.5). Answers queue in a queue of their own, **64 answers and 1 MiB charged**: when it is full the **oldest** answers are dropped and `GetNotice::Lagged { missed }` is told in their place. The server is never made to wait, and a slow reader of answers delays no kept item or emission.
5. **Duplicates** (§17.4). An answer is never merged with a kept item or an emission, and two servers' answers are two items.
6. **The types.** `GetOptions` is no longer `Copy` (it holds an `Ask`; it is `Clone`, `Debug`, `PartialEq` and `Eq`): pass a clone where a copy was passed. `Kind` and `GetNotice` are `#[non_exhaustive]` and gained the variants above: match them with a wildcard arm.
7. **Dropping the `Items` cancels the query**: its Interests are dropped, its pending slots released and a late answer discarded; a handler already running is not told.
8. **Which path an answer came by.** `Answered::via` is `Signed` for an answer of the signed path and **`Sealed`** for one of the channel path (the `Ask` mode, §3.7; `Ask::via`, `Auto` by default, chooses per server: [query.md](query.md) §3.2, §15.8). **Nothing in the stream changes with the path**: the order of §17.3, the counting of item 1, the slow reader's queue and the end of a query-only stream are the same, and an answer of the channel path is one `Kind::Answered` item like any other, with `by` the person of the key that signed the **session** (judged now), `writer` the server's device and `proof` `None`. `QueryFailed` gains `NoSession` and `Congested` there ([query.md](query.md) §3.3). `get` still asks no sender for a session of a channel to join (§17.1): a `QueryPath::Channel` query asks for an `Ask` session itself, and `GetOptions::modes` never joins one (no `Modes` value names `Ask`).

## 18. Performance pass

Informative, except that it names the two behaviours the pass changed and states them where they live (§12: `deliver_frame` hands the executor back once per 64 KiB; §14.10: the datagram limit is read from the sinks). It records what was measured after stage 7 against the owner's gates, what was found and changed, and what is left, with the number for each. The raw tables, the profiles and the commands are in the design note (SHAPES-CHANNEL.md §22). It replaces the verdicts of §16.6 and §16.7.

### 18.1 Method

- **Trees.** Before: ndn-rs `14466d3c`, ndn-ext `13c62b0` (the pins). After: ndn-rs `236795b3` (`818d3e03` differs by a doc comment), ndn-ext `22b8e47`. Release builds, `chan_ping`, `chan_relay`, `side_ping` and `carrier_bench` of `ndn-face-quic`.
- **Gates** (the owner's), over one QUIC link on loopback at `path_mtu_max` 9,000, against raw quinn on the same link and Zenoh on this machine: 8 B and 64 B throughput at least Zenoh's; 64 B half round trip at most 1.1 times the raw datagram, cold and hot; 1 KiB throughput at least 0.9 times the raw datagram; 8 KiB at least 0.9 times the raw stream.
- **Pairs.** Cores 10 to 13 only (two L3 complexes; their SMT siblings 42 to 45 belong to the host's other tenants), 3 s a row, channel, raw row and Zenoh run back to back in pairs, the order rotating each pair; 18 pairs a throughput row, 14 a latency row (cold, and hot with a spinner on each core: `sh` loops as in §16.1 and `yes` loops, which is Zenoh's own method). Ratios are formed per pair and then medianed; ranges are over pairs. Zenoh is the `Z1` row of a scratch copy of ndf's `versus` harness, run in the same pairs, so the Zenoh gate is judged **at the load of the channel's run**; its figures from the quiet host (1.98 M, 1.94 M msg/s) are given beside.
- **Load.** The host is shared and was busy: before, 1-minute load 30 to 57, with other tenants on cores 10 to 13 (26 to 64 %) and their siblings (38 to 76 %) at the start; after, 12 to 40, mostly 14 to 20, with those cores 1 to 6 % busy except for two short bursts. The two runs are **not at the same load**: the channel's absolute rates were about half of the earlier quiet-host ones before the pass; Zenoh's were 0.74 to 0.92 of its quiet-host ones. Only same-pair ratios, the same-load Zenoh column and CPU time per message mean anything, and the spreads are wide (pair ratios over 25 % on every throughput row).
- **Profiles.** `perf record` and `perf stat` (user space only: the host's `perf_event_paranoid` is 2; hardware counters work), the system share of each thread for the kernel, `strace -c -f` for system calls, and per-message CPU from `chan_ping only=cpu`. Instruction counts and CPU nanoseconds per message are used where wall rates are not resolvable.

### 18.2 The gates, before and after

Medians of 18 pairs (14 for latency); [min, max] over pairs. Zenoh is the same pairs' Zenoh.

| row | before | after | ratio before → after | gate | verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 8 B throughput | 1.207 M [0.43, 1.81] | **2.580 M** [1.66, 2.71] | to Zenoh 0.68 → **1.32** [0.82, 2.04] (Zenoh 1.47 M → 1.97 M) | ≥ Zenoh | miss → **pass** (both halves: 1.25, 1.33) |
| 64 B throughput | 1.022 M [0.57, 1.54] | **1.960 M** [1.09, 2.12] | to Zenoh 0.65 → **1.02** [0.58, 1.64] (Zenoh 1.46 M → 1.93 M) | ≥ Zenoh | miss → **marginal pass** (halves 0.95 and 1.03: a coin flip about the gate) |
| 1 KiB throughput | 123 k [93, 538] | 589 k [270, 632] | to the raw datagram 0.13 → **0.47** (0.49 with the raw row in a process of its own) | ≥ 0.9 | **miss** |
| 8 KiB throughput | 39 k [23, 85] | 79 k [24, 90] | to the raw stream 0.35 → **0.53** (0.65 with the raw row in its own process) | ≥ 0.9 | **miss** |
| 64 B half round trip, cold | 51.6 µs [43.7, 96.3] | 43.8 µs [33.7, 48.3] | to the raw datagram 1.57 → **1.36** [1.08, 1.64] | ≤ 1.1 | **miss** |
| 64 B half round trip, hot (`sh` spinners) | 70.1 µs | 43.7 µs | 1.71 → **1.19** [1.09, 1.50] | ≤ 1.1 | **miss** |
| 64 B half round trip, hot (`yes` spinners) | 63.6 µs | 42.2 µs | 1.81 → **1.33** [1.04, 1.59] | ≤ 1.1 | **miss** |

- **8 B and 64 B are the channel's own gain.** The controls moved too (Zenoh 1.34 and 1.32 times, the raw datagram 1.26 and 1.20 times between the two runs; the channel 2.1 and 1.9 times), so about 1.6 times at 8 B and 1.5 times at 64 B is the channel's: the receiver was the limit (§18.3).
- **1 KiB and 8 KiB: the rise is mostly the host.** Both rows are bimodal per run (§18.4). Inside the good mode the ratio to the raw row did not move (1 KiB 0.51 → 0.49, 8 KiB 0.61 → 0.59); what changed is how many pairs were in the good mode (2 of 18 → 10 of 18 and 3 of 18 → 9 of 18), and that follows the load of the cores' siblings (§18.4). Read the verdicts, not the rise, from these two rows. The baseline's good-mode figures rest on 2 and 3 pairs.
- **The face alone** (`side_ping`) is 1.03 to 1.06 times the raw datagram, before and after: the link adds 3 to 6 %, the channel the rest (channel over face 1.31 cold, 1.20 hot `sh`, 1.30 hot `yes`; before 1.52, 1.89, 1.73).
- **Raw rows in their own process** give the same 1 KiB datagram figure as in one process (1.225 M against 1.242 M), so rows contaminating one another is small here.

### 18.3 What was found and changed

Each change is one commit, with the test that failed first where the old code was wrong, and an invariant test where it was only slow. Wire format, signed content, checkpoint window, seal, replay and verification are unchanged.

| commit (ndn-rs) | cause | effect | test |
|---|---|---|---|
| `fddb7a93` | `Verify::forget_farthest` walked every slot from the highest down for each frame the 2 MiB bound forgot once a channel held 4,096 slots: 14.1 % of all samples at 64 B (29 % of the receiver thread), 4 to 5 % at 1 KiB and 8 KiB | a set of run starts kept on insert and removal makes it O(log n), the same slot chosen. 64 B receiver CPU 941 → 631 ns a delivered message (with the next); the 64 B half round trip cold 66 → 45 µs (after about 4,000 messages every frame had paid a ~20 µs walk) | invariants: the run start is kept for frames spaced wider than the window in total; the starts always equal a walk over the slots (6,000 random offers, forgets, judges) |
| `73baeb72` | `Verify::judge` re-walked and re-allocated every settled slot below the range at each checkpoint (up to about 4,000; 30.6 % of the receiver's user samples at 1,000 messages/s) | visits only the slots that still hold an unjudged frame, in place; receiver checkpoint cost +2.96 → +0.56 % of a core at 1,000/s (§18.6) | invariants: verdicts unchanged, counts recounted after every step |
| `7af97c13` | `Heap::emit` hashed the space's name and took a lock for every emission (`Tx::get`, 6.4 % of the sender thread at 64 B, with the hashing 12 %), and `enqueue` asked the carrier for the path's size on every message (two connection locks) | `Heap::emit` keeps the sender of a space (validated by `Tx::epoch`) and the channel keeps the datagram room the carrier last gave; sender 716 → 598 ns a message (`only=cpu`) | the room cache: **failed first** with the cache off (200 emissions asked 201 times); a kept sender is never used after the node replaced it; a path that grew takes the message the kept size refused as a datagram |
| `ffaeadea` | 17 reference counts per emission, 8 of them not kept | none taken that is not kept (sender borrowed, carrier kept by generation, payload moved, name octets referenced only where read); 598 → 474 ns (`next_batch` 596 → 405); 8 B sender 566 → 377 ns an emission | the standing-clear test **failed first** (left 2, right 1); the payload is taken only when queued |
| `9067a22c` | `attach_forwarder()` fixed every datagram frame at 1,200 octets (§14.10): on a link of 8,962 a message over about 1,150 went on the stream and a 1 KiB message filled a frame alone | the limit is the smallest live `side_frame_max` of the route's sinks (never over the node's own face or the host's ceiling): 1 KiB direct 150 k → 273 k a second, behind one relay 85 k → 142 k, sender CPU 8.5 → 5.3 µs a delivered message | **failed first** (`16 messages of 3,000 octets took Carried { datagrams: 0, stream_frames: 33 }`, link limit 8,962); a frame over a live limit is refused whole and counted |
| `c150061c` | the face reader never let a link's task run between the frames of a burst (relay fan-out is non-blocking, one worker), so the link's buffer filled and the rest was refused | the reader yields once after a refused frame: relay frames out per frame in 0.88 → 0.99; 64 B at 4,096 paths +15 % | **failed first** (`sink_full: 50` of 50) |
| `a7fb401d` | the slot store did about 14 tree searches a frame | 3 (in order, no neighbour search; `pop_first`): −1.3 k user instructions a frame, 6 % of a hop | invariants |
| `71ac6779`, `82ea75c3`, `c5809bb8`, `bfedc134` | per-message moves and atomics on the receiver (Message 120 B, Out 192 B, Got 160 B; two atomics) | messages parsed into the frame's vector, a queued message replaced in place, one move from `Out` to the queue: −173 instructions a message (12 % of the receiver); receiver 8 B 468 → 404 ns, 64 B 641 → 502 ns a delivered message | `parse_into` gives what the iterator gives (and the malformed-plaintext table); the held and `Verified` tests |
| `236795b3` | the 1 KiB and 8 KiB bad mode (§18.4): the host's read loop ran more than 1 MiB ahead of the consumer on a slow receiver's worker, so the `Latest` bound evicted messages already opened and checked | `deliver_frame` hands the executor back once per 64 KiB; a receiver that cannot keep up now sheds frames in the link's datagram queue, before any decryption. 1 KiB, receiver core slowed: 102 k → 311 k; natural 135 k → 305 k (12 pairs); 8 KiB slowed 27.7 k → 37.8 k; the bad mode's backlog (59 k datagrams, 506 MiB resident in 3 s) → 0 to 34 datagrams, 22 MiB | **failed first** (`missed == 0`: 79 evicted of 1,051 carried) |

ndn-ext: `8e5c0bf` (`chan_ping` and `chan_relay` take `trust=prompt|verified`), `e1f06d5` (the forwarder-attached datagram-size tests), `7e0a89d` (`chan_relay relay_workers=<n>`), `22b8e47` (`LinkHandle::quic_stats`, read only; the thr rows print the link's counters).

### 18.4 The cause of each remaining miss

**1 KiB and 8 KiB throughput: two AEAD passes on one worker.** The raw rows' sender CPU is 0.98 µs a 1 KiB datagram (0.88 of a core at 897 k a second) and 6.3 µs an 8 KiB stream message. The channel adds, on the sender, one more AES-256-GCM pass over the frame (0.32 and 2.6 µs at 3.2 GB/s a core: one stitched `ring` kernel, 42 to 46 % of the sender thread at these sizes, the two passes not separable by symbol), one copy of the payload into the frame (0.1 and 0.8 µs), the hash of each sent frame for the checkpoint (0.15 and 0.5 µs: the hash is part of the signed content) and its own emit path (about 0.3 µs): the ratio **cannot exceed 0.63 at 1 KiB and 0.62 at 8 KiB** even with the emit path free, and the gate needs the extra work under 0.1 times the raw cost. The receiver pays the same second pass (AES open 47 to 53 % of its user time) and two copies of each frame. Measured in the good mode: 0.49 to 0.51 and 0.59 to 0.61 (0.65 at 8 KiB with the raw row in its own process). The old estimate of the ceiling (0.61 and 0.72 to 0.82, §16.3) was of the same order.

- **`workers=2` does not help.** Emitter and driver on separate workers made both rows and the raw rows worse on this host: channel 1 KiB 339 to 459 k → 188 to 244 k, raw datagram 1.07 to 1.26 M → 0.53 M, channel 8 KiB 44 to 84 k → 24 to 52 k, raw stream 138 k → 97 k (2 pairs each): a hand-over costs more than the pass it hides, as in the two offload designs of §16.5.
- **The 8 KiB row is on the datagram lane** at `path_mtu_max` 9,000 (284 k datagram frames against 172 stream frames in 4 s); the stream lane carries checkpoints and binds only (about 45 frames a second), so the 4-octet prefix copy of ndn-ext's `side.rs` and the stream reader's copy cannot move a gated row and were not changed.
- **The bimodality** (1 KiB 93 to 540 k, 8 KiB 23 to 90 k in the baseline) is the receiver's core being slower than the sender's: the receiver thread's IPC was 2.1 to 2.3 in the good runs and 1.4 to 1.7 in the bad ones, rate and IPC tracked the load of the receiver core's SMT sibling (9 to 14 % busy: 74 to 79 k at 8 KiB; 39 to 86 %: 26 to 55 k), and a spinner on the receiver's sibling alone forced the bad mode at 1 KiB (103 k median) while one on the sender's gave a slow row with full frames (355 k). There was no UDP loss in the kernel, no QUIC loss, no congestion event and a congestion window of 100 to 600 MB in both modes: the sender cannot be throttled by flow control (datagrams have none) and a slower receiver simply delivers fewer. What the code did wrong in the bad mode is the read loop of `deliver_frame` (§18.3, `236795b3`); what remains is the host. Benchmark threads on cores of one L3 pair measured better than on two (45 to 84 k against 18 to 24 k at 8 KiB, 3 runs each).

**64 B half round trip: the cold working set of the whole hop.** The face alone is 1.03 to 1.06 times the raw datagram; the channel adds about 12 µs a hop (about 35 k cycles) on cores where raw takes 31 µs. Counted per hop on both threads (user space, `chan_ping only=lat` against `carrier_bench only=lat`):

| per hop | channel | raw |
|---|---|---|
| user cycles | 56 to 61 k | 21 to 27 k |
| user instructions | 43 k | 21 to 26 k |
| L1d load misses | 1,756 to 2,033 | 927 to 1,230 |
| L2 misses (`cache-misses`) | 2,987 to 4,707 | 1,230 to 2,484 |
| L1i misses | 171 to 524 | 121 to 300 |

A channel hop touches about 2,000 cache lines (130 KB) against 59 KB for raw, with a 32 KB L1d: the code runs cold, at an IPC of about 0.7 for the hop. Where the extra cycles are: quinn +6.7 k (the same code at IPC 0.9 against 1.5, because the channel pushes its lines out), unresolved symbols +7.9 k, the channel's **receiver** +4.8 k (14 %: `dispatch` 32 L1d lines, half of them tree nodes of `Verify`; the reader closure 39; `Sink::deliver` 22; `process` 19), the channel's **sender** +3.3 k (135 L1d and 223 L2 misses a hop: reference counts 12.6 and 10.6, mutex words, the stack copies of `Packed` and `Pending`), tokio and locks +2.7 k, allocation and copies +2.3 k (about 9 allocations a hop on both threads), AES +1.75 k, checkpoint crypto 0.8 k, the face layer 0.6 k. **No single piece is more than about 1.6 µs** (removing the receiver's channel code entirely) and the gate needs about 8 to 10 µs: it takes a smaller working set for the whole hop (one runtime for the reader and the connection driver, busy-polling before the reader parks), which are design changes. The per-ping `tokio::time::timeout` of the harness, which the raw rows lack, makes no difference (`timer=0`: 42.75 against 38.4 µs, 10 pairs). The half round trip is also **bimodal per binary** on this host by ±8 µs (it was in the earlier passes: 32 to 33 µs against 40 to 47 µs a process), which is larger than any change the code can show; 14 pairs cannot resolve a 0.1 to 1 µs change, so the changes of this size (a flat sorted vector in place of `Verify`'s two tree sets, about 17 L1d lines and 0.1 to 0.15 µs; the sender's hot fields in one line; a lone-`Latest` fast path, about 400 instructions) were **not made**. The two counts of the table (the sender's and the receiver's own runs) differ in the L2 figure for the same reason the ranges are wide: the host.

**64 B throughput: marginal.** The sender emits 1.8 to 2.5 M a second (491 ns an emission at 64 B, 377 ns at 8 B); the receiver, at 500 to 630 ns a delivered message at 64 B and 404 to 470 ns at 8 B, is the limit, and the harness's consumer runs on its thread. Zenoh at 1.93 M at this load needs about 465 ns on the receiver's one worker. The remaining receiver items are each 4 to 15 % at 8 B (the queue offer 14.6 %, parse about 20 %, memmove 13 %, `poll_next` 6.4 %, the drop of `Got` 4.3 %, AES 3.9 %): flat. The public `Got` carried three `Name` clones (14 atomic operations a message in all); sharing `by` and `writer` as one `Arc` saves four of them, about 50 to 60 ns of about 590, and was built after this pass as an API change (§18.10 item 2).

**Frame fill is not a cause.** At 64 B the sender sends frames of about 4.4 KB (50 to 52 messages) against a limit of 8,962 because the harness's emitter passes over 256 paths and yields once, and the first two messages of a pass go out inline. Making frames larger (a message within 10 µs of the last inline write waits for the call back) raised the messages a frame from 50 to 63 and cut the frames a second by 25 to 38 %, and moved neither side's CPU a message (send 527 → 550 ns, receive 680 → 720 ns): per-frame cost is under 5 % of the per-message cost. It was removed.

**The relay: one more QUIC hop.** One relay hop adds about 28 µs to the half round trip (relay1 minus direct, 4 pairs: 60, 18, 28, 27 µs), which is what a raw QUIC datagram hop costs on the same cores at the same time (28 to 39 µs). Per round trip the relay thread adds 2.7 `sendmsg`, 5.3 `recvmmsg` (half of them find nothing) and 4.4 `epoll_wait`, and quinn sends a 36-octet acknowledgement for each packet **before** the data is forwarded. The channel's own code on the relay (the side table `on_frame`, the face reader, the link face) is about 6 % of the relay thread's user samples; `SideTable::on_frame` is 0.15 % of all samples: under 1 µs of the 28. At 1 KiB the relay thread saturates (it decrypts and encrypts every 9 kB frame, AES-GCM 44 % of its user samples) and the kernel drops what it never read (the relay's frames in per frame out of the sender's link 0.46 to 0.55 at one worker); a relay with two workers (`relay_workers=2`, on four cores that ratio is 0.83; with every runtime on two workers on six cores it is 0.95 and 1 KiB behind the relay 160 k → 440 k; a daemon relay on a multi-thread runtime is not bound this way). Relay 64 B throughput (0.33 of direct after) is conflation of `Latest` over 256 paths: the same 52 messages a frame leave the sender in both shapes, behind the relay they arrive in bursts and the receiver keeps one value a path (at 4,096 paths relay1 delivers 905 k against direct 960 to 1,050 k).

### 18.5 Options that need the owner's decision

None of these was built. Each names what it trades.

| option | numbers | property traded, and what changes for a user |
|---|---|---|
| Stripe a link over several QUIC connections | the connection is the ceiling: four independent connections gave 15.8 Gbit/s at 1 KiB and 18.2 Gbit/s at 8 KiB (about linear, SHAPES-CHANNEL.md §17.2); each has its own worker, so the two AEAD passes run in parallel | strict ordering and fate-sharing between stripes (`Latest` tolerates it, `Reliable` and `Window` would need a sequence merge on the receiver); memory and handshakes a link times the stripes. The only way to the 1 KiB and 8 KiB gates on this CPU. |
| A CPU with VAES (Zen 3, Ice Lake and later) | `ring`'s AES-GCM is about three times faster there, for both passes and the raw rows | nothing; the gates are judged on the host they are measured on |
| AES-128-GCM for the channel's seal | about 10 of 14 rounds, GHASH unchanged: a modest gain, [INFERENCE: not measured]; does not close a gap of 1.6 times | the key length, 256 → 128 bits, for every sealed frame; a new `S_AEAD` code |
| Hash a frame for the checkpoint off the sender's thread | 5 % of the sender thread at 64 B, 7 to 8 % at 1 KiB and 8 KiB; cannot reach a gate alone | needs a spare core, a thread hand-over every 20 ms and a wasm fallback; same signed content |
| The gates at 1 KiB and 8 KiB | one worker with two AEAD passes does 0.5 to 0.6 times the raw row (ceiling 0.62) | the gate, not the code: 0.5 times raw, or the raw row plus one more AEAD pass |
| quinn acknowledgement frequency (`ack_eliciting_threshold` 10) | the raw hop's p50 39.0 → 27.3 µs (5 pairs); in `chan_relay` direct 47.1 → 42.7 and relay1 73.9 → 68.2 µs, 1 KiB throughput not better (inside the spread) | loss recovery waits up to `max_ack_delay` |
| A relay with more than one worker | `relay_workers=2` on four cores: frames out per frame in 0.5 → 0.83; 64 B relay1 438 k → 580 k; all runtimes at two workers on six cores: 1 KiB 160 k → 440 k | deployment: a daemon relay on a multi-thread runtime is already not bound this way |
| The first hop's datagram limit only | a relay whose own link takes less than the sender's first hop refuses a frame (counted `sink_error`); a signal back to the sender is a wire change | hosts set a ceiling with `attach_forwarder_with(n)` |
| Busy-poll before the reader parks, or one runtime for the reader and the driver | the latency excess is the cold working set of a hop (§18.4); neither was built or measured | a core burnt; or a change of how a host runs its link |
| `Verified` at line rate | back to back it delivers 0.6 % of the messages at 64 B and none at 1 KiB, because kept frames and held bodies charge one 2 MiB share twice, so frames are forgotten before their checkpoint arrives; nothing unverified is ever delivered | raise `CHANNEL_BYTES` and `NODE_BYTES` (for example 16 MiB and 64 MiB), or charge kept plus held once: receiver memory only |
| An out-of-place AEAD open | removes one copy of each frame (5 to 8 % of the receiver's samples at 1 KiB and 8 KiB) | `ring` 0.17.14 has none; pinning the shared receive buffer lets a key holder amplify memory |

### 18.6 Reported rows

**The relay** (`chan_relay`, 3 repetitions in one process, medians; both shapes got faster, direct more than the relay, so the ratios are worse): 64 B half round trip direct 73.7 → 36.1 µs, one relay 91.8 → 73.6 µs (1.25 → 2.04 times; p99 145 → 50.5 and 400 → 110.5 µs); 1 KiB half round trip 80.7 → 38.8 and 120.6 → 80.0 µs; 64 B throughput 1.18 M → 1.87 M direct and 507 k → 622 k through the relay (0.43 → 0.33); 1 KiB 92.7 k → 531 k and 100 k → 193 k (1.08 → 0.36). The load differs (36 to 40 before, 15 after).

**Checkpoints** (`chan_relay` direct, paced 64 B, 5 s, 4 interleaved pairs; the cost is the 20 ms period minus the 2,000 ms period, in percentage points of one core; target 0.5 on each side):

| rate | sender before → after | receiver before → after |
|---|---|---|
| 100 a second | +0.32 → **+0.37** | +0.79 → **+0.52** [0.46, 0.71] |
| 1,000 a second | +0.34 → **+0.45** | +2.83 → **+0.69** [0.31, 0.86] |

The sender is within the target. The receiver is at it at 100 a second and 1.4 times over at 1,000. What is left is the verification of the signature: Ed25519 is about 78 µs a checkpoint in vivo with cold caches (0.39 % of a core at the 50 a second of the 20 ms period; 44.5 µs hot in a stand-alone benchmark, 0.22 %), and the stream path's system calls, acknowledgement and queue about 0.15 to 0.2 %. Reaching 0.5 % at 1,000 a second takes a longer period, which is a window change (§15.9), or a batch verification (23 µs a signature in a batch of 8, which a 20 ms period does not offer). The sender's cost is the hash of each frame and one signature a period, about 0.2 % of a core by arithmetic and +0.37 to +0.45 measured.

**`Trust::Verified` against `Prompt`.** CPU per message is the same within noise (receiver paced 1,000 a second: 4.10 % against 3.98 % of a core before; 5.16 % against 5.16 % after; at 10,000 a second 6.70 % against 6.82 %): `Verified` costs a `Body::clone` a frame. The half round trip is **21.5 ms** (before 22.4 ms) against 40.6 µs for `Prompt`: a `Verified` frame waits for the checkpoint that covers it, and the period is 20 ms. Back to back it delivers 12 k messages a second at 64 B (0.6 %) and none at 1 KiB, and at 10,000 a second paced 29 % (`Latest` shows a consumer the newest value of each path once a checkpoint, at most paths times 50 a second; and at 1 KiB and over the share of §18.5). Nothing unverified is delivered in any of these.

### 18.7 Tried and removed

- **Frame fill** (§18.4): fewer, larger frames; no CPU moved.
- **An inlined take chain** and **`&mut Message` through accept and resolve** on the receiver: the first lowers instructions by about 72 a message and does not show in nanoseconds (kept for the count, with the rest of `c5809bb8`); the second changed nothing (9.375 G against 9.398 G instructions). A per-frame precomputed charge in place of a division per message saved 8 instructions a message.
- **A harness reader-yield knob**, after the library change (`236795b3`) made it redundant.
- **quinn's acknowledgement frequency** (§18.5): not kept.

### 18.8 What the figures do not cover, and the checks

- Every run is loopback on one host shared with other tenants; the two runs of §18.2 differ in load, so the rise of the two bimodal rows cannot be attributed to the code, and the 64 B pass is a coin flip about the gate. `Reliable` and `Window` were not measured. No radio and no real interface.
- Not re-measured against a stage-3 build: whether the 0.86 to 0.91 of §16.7 is entirely `Verify`'s walks.
- The checks of the pass: `cargo test --workspace` in ndn-rs and in ndn-ext; clippy `--workspace --all-targets -D warnings` in both; `cargo fmt --check`; `cargo doc --workspace --no-deps` with `-Dwarnings`; the dependency guard and the retired-names guard; `cargo check --target wasm32-unknown-unknown` of the CI crate list; the full e2e (the forwarder and the app connection changed); the data conformance once clean and once faulty with `--check-determinism` (93 of 93 identical alone and in the suite, both).

### 18.9 Security review of the pass

One read-only review over every commit of §18.3 (ndn-rs `14466d3c..` and ndn-ext `13c62b0..`), with the questions the pass raised. **No finding** in: the O(log n) `forget_farthest`, the `judge` change and the in-order frame (the run-start set, the pending set and the slot map change together in `offer`, `conflict`, `judge`, `forget_slot` and `release_all`; the slot forgotten is the one the old walk chose, including ties and gaps of exactly the window; every subtraction is ordered; a checkpoint's verdict for every frame and the silence clock are those of the old code, and the 6,000-step test compares them with a walk); the sender's caches (a kept sender is given only while `Tx::epoch` and the trust generation stand, the carrier is stored before its generation moves, a name id is touched under the lock that peeked it); `parse_into` (the same checks and the same errors as the iterator, the caller clears the vector before and after, no allocation from a count in the header); the receiver's wake-ups (a waker is registered and the queue read again, so none is lost) and in-place replacement (the same bounds and counts); and `deliver_frame`'s yield (it comes after `chan_deliver` has committed the replay window, the ring, the counters, the evidence, any bar and any end, so a dropped future loses only the `Delivery` it would have returned; its two callers, the examples' read loops, neither time it out nor select on it).

| finding | status |
|---|---|
| **Medium.** One sink's datagram limit lowered every sink's frames, with no floor (`9067a22c`): a QUIC peer on a sink link chooses the datagram size it declares, and the route's limit was the minimum over the sinks. | **Fixed:** a sink counts for at least `SINK_LIMIT_FLOOR` (1,150) in a node's limit, the host's ceiling still lowering it; a sink below it refuses its own copy, counted. Test `one_sink_that_takes_next_to_nothing_does_not_shrink_the_frames_of_the_others` **failed first** (left 100, right 1150). The doc of `limits::datagram_limit` said the result was never below `MIN_FRAME_LEN`, which the code never did; corrected. |
| **Medium.** quinn's endpoint-to-connection queue is unbounded and the 64 KiB yield helps only hosts that call `deliver_frame`. | **Residual** (§13.12 item 35): quinn's queue, no structural bound possible outside quinn; the sentences of §12 and §18.3 that say a slow receiver sheds before any decryption hold under cooperative scheduling only. |
| **Low.** A default `attach_forwarder()` sizes frames to the first hop only. | **Residual** (§13.12 item 36). |
| **Low.** A full sink makes its source face's reader yield per refused frame. | **Residual** (§13.12 item 37). |
| **Info.** A kept datagram room that the path outgrew downward while the channel was idle let a message through to a carrier with no stream, which then dropped it (`lost_closed`) instead of the emission being refused `TooLarge` as before the pass. | **Fixed:** where a message cannot go on a stream (no stream, or a receiver behind a hop with none), the room is asked of the carrier, not taken from the kept one; the cache still serves every emission that has a stream, where a stale room costs one frame's repack. Test `a_path_that_shrank_while_idle_refuses_a_message_it_can_no_longer_take_where_there_is_no_stream` **failed first** (expected a refusal, got `Ok`). |
| **Info.** The sink limit is read under the sender's state lock for every frame (up to 64 sinks, one connection lock each). | No finding: no code that calls back into the heap runs under those locks. |

`attach_forwarder_with(n)` is a host API with no wire input; the limit is `min(own face, sinks (at least the floor, at most `n`), n)`, with no subtraction. The ndn-ext knobs (`trust=`, `relay_workers=`, `timeline`) are example arguments; `LinkHandle::quic_stats` returns quinn's counters of one connection (round trip, window, MTU, packets and bytes), no address or key, in fixed memory.

### 18.10 The four software items (REPLY-26, the owner's ruling on the per-message work)

After the pass the owner ruled (REPLY-26 §1 and §3, item 2 of the order) that the per-message software work which every host gains from is built, with no reliance on hardware acceleration. Four items, all built; the three that §18.5 listed as options (the decoded key, `Got`'s `Arc`, the smaller `Message`) are no longer options. What each keeps and what its test pins:

1. **The checkpoint hash is taken when the ring is drained.** An audit found the sender already did this: the ring holds the frames and hashes them only when the stream writer drains it for a checkpoint (`take_below`, the same task, no lock held), and no other frame hash is on the send path. What the checkpoint signs and proves is unchanged, and a test now says so: `a_checkpoint_hashed_when_the_ring_is_drained_is_the_record_an_eager_hash_signs` compares a real sender's checkpoint record, signature included, with `checkpoint::sign` over the same frames hashed eagerly with `sha2` and `blake3` directly (frames of both hash classes, up to 2,048 octets and above), and verifies it. (ndn-rs `28e464ab`.) The off-thread option of §18.5 stays an option.
2. **`Got` carries one `Arc<Author>`** (`Author { by, writer }`, §17.6), not two `Name` clones. The receive channel builds it once when the session is taken; the answer path and the kept feed build one for each answer or item. Test `the_emissions_of_one_session_share_one_author` (`Arc::ptr_eq` over three emissions). A public change: callers read `got.author.by` and `got.author.writer`. (ndn-rs `6e8ee9cb`; ndn-ext, ndn-fwd and ndn-sim follow in their repos.)
3. **`Message` is 64 octets**, asserted at compile time (it was 120), and a frame is parsed straight into the receiver's buffer: the names a frame carries sit beside the messages in `Parsed.names` and a message holds its name's index; `parse_into(&mut messages, &mut names)`. The refusals are **identical** to the old reader's, which the tests keep as the reference (`message_equivalence_tests.rs`): golden vectors, the malformed table, frames over 9,000 octets, a fixed-seed corpus of 60,000 cases and a deterministic table of every boundary (below), comparing messages, charges, share-or-copy and the exact refusal of both the iterator and `parse_into`. (ndn-rs `030473ad`; the review's follow-up `e1699942`.)
4. **The sender's Ed25519 key is decoded once a session** (`ndn_security::Ed25519PublicKey`), when the session is installed or renewed (`Signing`); a renewal with the same octets keeps the decoded key, a renewal with other octets replaces it, and octets that are not a point make every checkpoint fail as before. `Record::verify` takes the decoded key. Tests `a_decoded_key_answers_as_the_per_call_verifier_does` and `the_senders_checkpoint_key_is_decoded_once_and_kept_across_a_renewal`. (ndn-rs `17e4f728`; the one-lock renewal below, `1a56dbf9`.)

**Review follow-up (the Gate26 security review of this pass).** What the review found short in items 3 and 4, closed:

- **Corpus (item 3).** The equivalence tests now also run a deterministic table of every boundary of the format (`every_boundary_of_the_format_reads_and_refuses_as_the_old_reader_did`, about 500 cases), each against the reference and both production entries (messages, charges, share-or-copy, the exact refusal) and each stating the outcome the format gives it: every prefix of the golden plaintexts; the header count at n − 1, n and n + 1 with and without PAD; a payload at the limit −1, 0 and +1 (and the limit clamped to `MESSAGE_MAX`); names of 254, 255 and 256 octets; stand seconds 3599, 3600 and 3601; name ids 0, 1, 4096 and 4097; the original sequence at seq − 1, seq and seq + 1 in both flavours and in the second message of a frame; every one of the 256 flags octets; non-minimal 2-, 4- and 8-octet varints in every length field, at each boundary and cut inside their own octets; lengths that exceed the frame, in small frames and in frames of 9,000 and 9,001 octets; frames of 8,999 to 9,002 octets that carry named messages, padded and unpadded, and payloads at half of the frame. The table fails unless it reaches every refusal the reader has and every field a frame can end in. It found no refusal that differs from the old reader's. It did find a fault in the test: the share-or-copy comparison checked a payload against a buffer other than the one the readers read, so it never saw a shared payload; it now compares against the frame read, counts the shared and the copied payloads and requires both.
- **Names (item 3).** The reference reader shares every name with the frame; production copies a name out of a frame over 9,000 octets (`take_name`), so a name held by the name table for a bind never keeps a large frame alive (a name is at most 255 octets, never most of a large frame). That is intended and stays. The equivalence tests compare names **by value** only; sharing is compared with the reference for payloads, and for names it is checked against its own rule (`a_name_shares_a_small_frame_and_is_copied_out_of_a_large_one`: shared by a frame of at most 9,000 octets, copied out of a larger one, whatever the payload does).
- **Checked invariant (item 3).** `parse_into` numbers a frame's names from the first it writes, so it needs `names` empty; a buffer that is not empty is refused with `MessageError::NamesNotEmpty` before anything is read or written, instead of numbering from its length (an index is one octet and would wrap past 255). Test `parse_into_refuses_a_names_buffer_that_is_not_empty_and_writes_nothing`. The receive path clears the buffer before each frame, so the refusal is reached only by a caller's mistake.
- **One-lock renewal (item 4).** A renewal installs the sender's key, its checkpoint key and the new terms under one lock: `RecvSession::renewed` and `RecvSession::new` take the signer whole (`Signing { key, public }`), and nothing sets the checkpoint key apart. Before, a renewal took two locks, and a checkpoint verified between them used the old key and failed under it: the session ended, the face was barred and a forgery alert was raised on an honest sender that had rotated its key. Test `a_renewal_installs_the_rotated_checkpoint_key_with_the_renewal_and_not_after`.
- **A revoked sender key stops verifying.** The decoded key lives in the session and goes with it. The revocation reaches a live session through the trust generation, which `NodeInner`'s judge moves when the keyring adopts a context that revokes the key: the next frame, data or checkpoint, finds the session's verdict stale, judges the sender again (`RecvSession::recheck`, by `standing`), and a key that no longer stands ends the session `NotMember` before any frame is opened; the maintenance pass does the same between frames. Test `a_sender_key_revoked_mid_session_stops_its_next_frame_and_its_next_checkpoint`: a genuine data frame or checkpoint after the revocation is refused, nothing is delivered, no checkpoint is verified, and the `Items` are told.

**Measured effect: not measured apart.** PerfSoft did not measure the four items by themselves; their effect is folded into the first-hop pass's before and after table, the only measurement taken with them in. (`ndf`'s `versus` command on one host at a load of 12 to 19; N2 is `Latest`, N2R is `Reliable` with `Block`; messages a second at 8 B / 64 B / 1 KiB / 8 KiB.) The pin `e2e3256c` gives N2 513 k / 522 k / 152 k / 19.4 k (`ndf`'s figures). The tree at `030473ad`, which carries the four items **and stage 4's code** and none of the first-hop changes, gives N2 523 k / 553 k / 151 k / 19.5 k: inside run-to-run noise, so the items' 50 to 60 ns of about 590 a message at 8 B (about 10 %) is not resolved at these rates, and the table does not separate them from stage 4. The first-hop pass's own columns are N2 730 k / 675 k / 279 k / 48.5 k and N2R 2.00 M / 1.84 M / 656 k / 105.6 k (all delivered; before it N2R gave 1.86 M / 1.86 M / 154 k / 19.6 k at 96, 97, 17 and 12 % delivered): the work-conserving quota and the first-hop hold-back, not these items.
